Filtrations on quantum cohomology from the Floer theory of ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -actions
Alexander F. Ritter, Filip Živanović

Abstract
. We construct a filtration by ideals on quantum cohomology for symplectic manifolds with a Hamiltonian S 1 S^{1} -action that extends to a pseudoholomorphic ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -action. These spaces include all Conical Symplectic Resolutions, in particular all Quiver Varieties. In particular, we obtain a family of filtrations on singular cohomology for any Conical Symplectic Resolution, that is sensitive to the choice of ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -action. The symplectic form is rarely exact at infinity for these spaces, so substantial foundational work is carried out to rigorously define Floer theory, in particular symplectic cohomology. Using Floer theory, we construct a periodic persistence module, giving rise to a graded periodic barcode associated to the ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -action. This encodes birth-death phenomena of Floer invariants. Our filtrations can be viewed as a Floer-theoretic analogue of Atiyah–Bott filtrations, arising from stratifying a manifold by gradient flowlines of a Morse–Bott function, but they are distinct from those and they can detect non-topological properties of the quantum product.



1.
Overview of the results

1.1.
Motivation

We will apply Floer-theoretic techniques to describe the topology and cohomology of a large new class of open symplectic manifolds.
This class brings under one umbrella many interesting families of spaces: cotangent bundles of flag varieties,
negative complex vector bundles, Conical Symplectic Resolutions (CSRs),
Moduli spaces of Higgs bundles,
crepant resolutions of quotient singularities,
1
1

1

arising in the generalised McKay Correspondence.

and semiprojective toric manifolds. The family of CSRs itself includes:
ADE resolutions, hypertoric varieties, Nakajima quiver varieties, and Springer resolutions of Slodowy varieties.

To illustrate some features, recall that
a
weight-
s
CSR
is a projective
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant resolution
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}_{0}
of a normal affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, whose
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action contracts
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
to a point, and having a holomorphic symplectic structure
(\mathfrak{M},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
compatible with the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action:
t\cdot\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
.
These spaces are much studied in Geometric Representation Theory, whilst their symplectic topology is less investigated. All known examples of CSRs are hyperkähler: the complex structures
J,K
give rise to exact symplectic forms
\omega_{J}
,
\omega_{K}
making these Liouville manifolds. These have special exact Lagrangian submanifolds called minimal Lagrangians
[
Ž22
]
relevant to the A-side of Homological Mirror Symmetry.
In this paper we instead study directly the B-side: we consider the complex structure
I
with its typically non-exact Kähler form
\omega_{I}
.
These spaces are almost never convex at infinity,
2
2

2

See
8.11
.
so they have been out of reach of Floer-theoretic study so far.
The above minimal Lagrangians now become
I
-holomorphic
\omega_{I}
-symplectic submanifolds. These spaces are very rich in
I
-holomorphic curves, whereas there are no non-constant closed
J
- or
K
-holomorphic curves as
\omega_{J}
,
\omega_{K}
are exact.

The approach of studying cohomological invariants directly on the B-side using Floer theory was explored in the context of the cohomological McKay correspondence by McLean–Ritter
[
MR23
]
. That paper studied crepant resolutions
Y\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
of isolated quotient singularities, where a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
Y
plays a prominent role, but it was still a setup where
Y
is convex at infinity. Our original hope was that it should be fairly straightforward to adapt those constructions to quiver varieties, which are not convex at infinity, but still admit a very nice
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action. As it turned out, the endeavour was much harder than expected, so we ventured on to establish this foundational paper, of wider scope.

We call our class of spaces
symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds
as they admit a pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action

\varphi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Y\to Y,\;(t,y)\mapsto\varphi_{t}(y),

whose
S^{1}
-part is a Hamiltonian action.
More precisely, we have a symplectic manifold
(Y,\omega)
admitting an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
I
, with respect to which
\varphi
is pseudoholomorphic.

The moment map for the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-action is denoted

(1)

H:Y\to\mathbb{R}.

The subgroups
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
S^{1}
of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
yield commuting vector fields
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla H
and
X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}=IX_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
.
To control the behaviour of
Y
at infinity, we assume that
\varphi
is
contracting
: the
-\nabla H
flow of any point eventually lands in a prescribed compact subset. This is equivalent to requiring that
H
is bounded below and that the set
\mathfrak{F}=\mathrm{Crit}(H)
is compact. The subset
\mathfrak{F}\subset Y
is in fact the fixed locus of the action,
and its connected components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
are
I
-holomorphic and
\omega
-symplectic submanifolds of
Y.

\mathfrak{F}=\mathrm{Crit}(H)=Y^{S^{1}}=Y^{\mathbb{C}^{*}}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

There is a partition of
Y
by the stable manifolds
U_{\alpha}
of the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
under the
-\nabla H
flow, whereas the unstable manifolds
D_{\alpha}
partition a path-connected compact subset
\mathrm{Core}(Y)\subset Y
called the
core
.
Unlike
\mathfrak{F}
, the core can be quite singular. We prove that the inclusion recovers Čech cohomology,
H^{*}(Y)\cong\check{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Core}(Y))
, and in many reasonable situations we show this is singular cohomology and that
Y
deformation retracts onto
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
.
For weight-
1
CSRs,
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
is a singular
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian subvariety, and the minimal
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}:=\min H
from
[
Ž22
]
is a smooth irreducible component of it.

Although the inspiration for this paper originated from a combination of
[
MR23
,
Rit10
]
with the goal of studying quiver varieties, the above level of generality owes much to the algebro-geometrical analogue of such spaces, called semiprojective varieties,
which were first
investigated in detail by Hausel–Rodriguez-Villegas
[
HRV15
]
(building upon work by Simpson and Nakajima).

The topology of closed Kähler manifolds with Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions is well-understood, by Frankel
[
Fra59
]
, Atiyah–Bott
[

and Kirwan
[

]
. Using those methods we show that

is a Morse–Bott function, whose Morse–Bott submanifolds are the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and together with their (even) Morse–Bott indices
\mu_{\alpha}\geq 0
they determine the
cohomology of
Y
as a vector space (working over a field),
3
3

3

Here
A[d]
means we shift a graded group
A
down by

(A[d])_{n}=A_{n+d}
.

(2)

H^{*}(Y)\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}].

An intuitive explanation is that the unstable manifold of a cycle in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, which would normally have real codimension one boundary, has codimension two boundary due to the
S^{1}
-action, and can therefore be viewed as a cycle in
Y
. More generally, taking suitable unions of stable manifolds

of the

]

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.
We may assume

is connected, so only one summand

in (

, and it is
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}=\min H
.
It will be convenient to pick (
1
) so that
\min H=0
(by adding a constant to

), thus
H\geq 0
on
Y
.

The main goal of this paper is to construct a filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
by ideals on quantum cohomology
QH^{*}(Y)
, ordered by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We remark that there is an on-going interest in Algebraic Geometry in constructing filtrations on the cohomology of such spaces, for example Higgs moduli admit the “
P=W
” perverse filtration,
[
dCHM12
,
HMMS22
,
MS22
]
, and we will compare it with ours in
[
RŽ24b
]
.
Our construction instead relies on Floer theory: symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, whose chain level generators are loosely the
S^{1}
-orbits; and its positive version
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
, which ignores the constant
S^{1}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{F}
. We anticipate the big picture that relates them:

Theorem 1.1
.
4
4

4

The technical assumption, needed for Floer theory and to make sense of
QH^{*}(Y)
, is that
Y
satisfies a certain weak+ monotonicity property (
6.7
). This includes for example all non-compact Calabi-Yau and non-compact Fano

.

The canonical algebra homomorphism
c^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is surjective, equal to localisation at a
Gromov–Witten invariant

Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y),

where
\mu
is the Maslov index of
\varphi
,

SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)\cong QH^{*}(Y)/E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})\cong QH^{*}(Y)_{Q_{\varphi}},

where

E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})=\ker c^{*}\subset QH^{*}(Y)

is the generalised
0
-eigenspace of quantum product by
Q_{\varphi}
.
This yields a Floer-theoretic presentation of
QH^{*}(Y)
as a
\mathbb{K}
-module,

(3)

QH^{*}(Y)\cong SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\oplus SH^{*}(Y,\varphi).

Moreover, for
N^{+}\in\mathbb{R}
just above

, the continuation maps

(whose direct limit is

),

(4)

can be identified with quantum product
N

Q_{\varphi}
on

. In particular,

SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0\Leftrightarrow(\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)\textrm{ for some }\lambda<\infty)\Leftrightarrow(Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y)\textrm{ is nilpotent}),

which always occurs
5
5

5

See
1.22

if
c_{1}(Y)=0
including all CSRs, crepant resolutions of quotient singularities, and Higgs moduli spaces.

That localisation result generalises the main theorem from Ritter
[
Rit16
]
. The construction of

and its usage to compute symplectic cohomology, goes back to
[
Rit14
]
: see

[

[
Rit14
]

For CSRs,
QH^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(Y)
as algebras (with suitable coefficients),
so we obtain a
\varphi

by ideals with respect to cup-product, and
H^{*}(Y)\cong SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
.

In the sequel
[
RŽ24b
]
we use the foundational results from this paper to construct a Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence that converges to
QH^{*}(Y)
, whose
E_{1}
-page involves the cohomologies of Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits of the moment map (
1
) intersected with higher and higher level sets of
H
.
This can be interpreted as a Floer-theoretic generalisation of (
2
), which instead arises from a
Morse–Bott spectral sequence for ordinary Morse–Bott cohomology for the moment map.

In general, there is an obstacle to defining quantum cohomology, let alone Floer cohomology, due to the non-compactness of
Y
. The danger is the non-compactness of moduli spaces of PDE solutions which escape to infinity. The symplectic form
\omega
is
very rarely exact at infinity
for these spaces, indeed for CSRs almost always
6
6

6

Exceptions being
\mathbb{C}^{2n},
ADE resolutions and
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n}
, see
Section

8.2
.
one finds closed
I
-holomorphic curves appearing at infinity.

We, therefore, require one final condition, and in retrospect arriving at this definition was the decisive idea to open up this large class of examples to Floer-theoretic study going beyond just quiver varieties.
We say
Y
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold
over a convex base
if outide of a compact subset
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
, so on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}:=Y\setminus\mathrm{int}(Y^{\mathrm{in}})
, there is a pseudoholomorphic proper map

(5)

\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to B=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)

to the positive symplectisation of a closed contact manifold
\Sigma
,
such that
X_{S^{1}}
maps to the Reeb field,

(6)

\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{B}.

The existence of such a map
\Psi
automatically ensures that the action
\varphi
on
Y
is contracting.
We say
\Psi
is
globally defined
over a convex base
B
, if (
5
) extends to a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant pseudoholomorphic proper map

\Psi:Y\to B

to an open symplectic manifold
B
which is convex at infinity (e.g. a Liouville manifold), admitting a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action whose
S^{1}
-part generates the Reeb flow at infinity.

Remark 1.2
.

We do not require any conditions on the dimension of
B
, and
\Psi_{*}
is allowed to have a large kernel with varying ranks. We do not assume that
\Psi
is surjective, so
although the Reeb flow will be periodic on the image of
\Psi
, this need not hold everywhere on
B
.
One can allow a positive constant factor in (
6
), but after a rescaling argument we may assume (
6
) holds as written.
We caution the reader that level sets of
H
are in general not preserved by the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action, so level sets of
H
need not map into slices
\Sigma\times\{R\}
via
\Psi
(an exception
are cotangent bundles and negative vector bundles, Examples
1.8
and
1.9
). In (
6
),
\mathcal{R}_{B}=X_{R}
is a Hamiltonian vector field for the radial coordinate
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
.
In this paper, but not in
[
RŽ24b
]
, we can allow a slightly more general condition in (
6
):

(7)

\hskip 107.63855pt\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=X_{fR}\qquad\textrm{ for a Reeb-invariant function }f:\Sigma\to(0,\infty).

One can identify
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
with the complement of a disc bundle of a complex line bundle over an orbifold

\{H=const\}/S^{1}

(assuming
H
is proper), e.g. viewing it as a subset of the symplectic cut, however one usually cannot hope to use this as
B
: the identification is rarely pseudoholomorphic
[
Ler95
, Rmk.1.1]
.

1.2.
Examples of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds over a convex base

Example 1.3

(Equivariant projective morphisms)
.

Let
X
be an affine variety
7
7

7

Which is not equal to a point. This ensures
Y
is non-compact. We may assume
Y
is connected.

with a contracting
8
8

8

The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action contracts
X
to a single fixed point. Algebraically, the coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[X]
is
\mathbb{N}
-graded with
\mathbb{C}[X]_{0}=\mathbb{C}
.

algebraic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action. Any equivariant projective morphism

\pi:Y\rightarrow X
satisfies our assumptions, for a globally defined
\Psi

\mathbb{C}[X]
is
\mathbb{N}
-graded by the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action; we choose homogeneous generators

9
9

9

is proper because it is a projective morphism.

I

\Psi:Y\to X\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},\quad y\mapsto\pi(y)\mapsto(f_{1},\ldots,f_{N})|_{\pi(y)}\mapsto(f_{1}^{w/w_{1}},\ldots,f_{N}^{w/w_{N}})|_{\pi(y)},

that is
\mathbb{C}^{*}

with weight

The quasi-projective variety

10

The proof of these claims is the same as the proof written for CSRs in

.

a

Particular examples of this, which arise as equivariant projective resolutions, are

Example 1.4

(Kähler/GIT quotients of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
)
.

An instance of
1.3
are
Kähler reductions of unitary actions

K\subset U(V)
on a complex vector space
V
, or equivalently
(by the Kempf–Ness theorem) GIT quotients of the complexified actions
K_{\mathbb{C}}=:G\subset GL(V)
.
By the GIT picture, they always come with a projective morphism

\pi:Y=V/\!\!/_{\chi}G\rightarrow V/\!\!/_{0}G=X
to the affine GIT quotient, where
\chi:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
is a character.
The scalar matrices
\mathbb{C}^{*}\cong S\leq GL(V)
cannot be a subgroup of
G
, as otherwise

X
would be a single point,
11
11

11

Given any
z\in V,

the closure of its orbit
Gz\supset Sz
would contain the origin (
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}s\cdot z=0
),
thus by definition

[z]=[0]
in the GIT quotient
X.
which we do not allow (
Footnote

7
).
Hence, there is an induced
\pi
-equivariant action of
S
on
Y
and
X
, which is contracting.
12
12

12

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}s\cdot[z]=\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}[s\cdot z]=[0].

Particular instances of this are

semiprojective toric manifolds
, for which
G
is a torus
[
HS02
]
. We discuss them in detail in

Example 1.5

(Submanifolds)
.

Given any space
Y
from our class of spaces, any
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant properly embedded
I
-pseudoholomorphic submanifold

will also belong to our class.
13
13

13

The
I
-compatible form
j^{*}\omega
makes

symplectic; the
S^{1}
-action is Hamiltonian with moment map
H\circ j
; and the
\Psi
-map for

is
\Psi\circ j:S^{\mathrm{out}}:=S\cap j^{-1}(Y^{\mathrm{out}})\to\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
.

Example 1.6

(Blow-ups)
.

Our class of spaces is closed under blow-ups of compact subvarieties, provided that the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action lifts to the blow-up, since (
7
) is only a condition at infinity. In the toric case,
[
Rit16
, Sec.3E-3F]
described the effect of such blow-ups on quantum and symplectic cohomology.

Example 1.7

(
A_{2}
-singularity)
.

CSRs of the lowest dimension are ADE resolutions.
14
14

14

Minimal resolutions of quotient singularities
M\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma,
where
\Gamma\leq SL(2,\mathbb{C})
is a finite subgroup.
The
A_{2}
case is the minimal resolution
\pi:M\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/3)
(third roots of unity
\zeta
acting on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by
(x,y)\mapsto(\zeta x,\zeta^{-1}y)
). We can define

as the blow up at
0
of the image
V(XY-Z^{3})\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
of the embedding
\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/3)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}
,
[x,y]\mapsto(x^{3},y^{3},xy)
. The McKay
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on

is the lift of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
(x,y)\mapsto(tx,ty)
. The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant map
\Psi:M\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
is
(X^{2},Y^{2},Z^{3})

6
diagonal
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on

Example 1.8

(Springer resolutions)
.

A simple instance of
1.3
, and of a weight-1 CSR,
is

(8)

T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{N}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{min}}:=\{A\mid A^{2}=0,\ \text{rk}(A)=1\}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{N+1}.

Here
\varphi
is the standard
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action that contracts fibres, and
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts by dilation on
\mathfrak{sl}_{N+1}.
The zero section of
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{N}
is
I
-holomorphic and
\omega
-symplectic, so
\omega
is not the canonical exact form of
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{N}
.

A more general class of such examples are Springer resolutions, i.e. cotangent bundles of flag varieties. For example, the variety

\mathcal{B}
of all flags
0\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
:
there is a resolution of singularities

\nu:T^{*}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{N}=\{3\times 3\textrm{ nilpotent matrices in }\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\}
.
The singular affine variety
\mathcal{N}
has three strata: (i) the point
\mathcal{O}_{1,1,1}:=0

to which
\mathcal{N}
contracts to under the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action; (ii) the stratum
\mathcal{O}_{2,1}
which is the adjoint orbit
of the nilpotent Jordan normal form with blocks of sizes 2,1; and (iii) a generic stratum
\mathcal{O}_{3}
.
The orbit
\mathcal{O}_{2,1}
is a singular stratum of
\mathcal{N}
that goes to infinity. Any transverse slice to
\mathcal{O}_{2,1}

(entering from the generic stratum, and avoiding
\mathcal{O}_{1,1,1}
) will be an
A_{2}
-singularity; its preimage via
\nu
gives its resolution; in particular the fibre above the chosen point in
\mathcal{O}_{2,1}
consists of two holomorphic
\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
’s intersecting transversely. This illustrates the general feature that, in our spaces
Y
,

I
-holomorphic spheres can appear arbitrarily far out at infinity within the fibres of the map (
5
).

Example 1.9

(Negative Vector Bundles)
.

In
Section

B.1
we explain why
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{N}
in
1.8
is a negative vector bundle. Other examples of negative vector bundles are the duals of ample vector bundles, e.g. conormal bundles of projective algebraic varieties
V\subset\mathbb{C}P^{N}
. One can also make a vector bundle negative by tensoring with a large power of a negative line bundle.
Following
[
Rit14
, Sec.11]
,
for a negative complex vector bundle
Y=\mathrm{Tot}(\pi:E\rightarrow B)
with the standard contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres, we use the biholomorphism
\Psi:E\setminus 0\to L\setminus 0
where
L\to{\mathbb{P}}(E)
is the tautological line bundle over the complex projectivisation of
E
. This
\Psi
typically does not extend globally, and
\Psi
is not symplectic when
\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{C}}E\geq 2
.
15
15

15

The natural symplectic form
\omega
on
E
is non-exact at infinity whereas for
L
it is.

This map was used in
[
Rit14
, Sec.11.2]
to define quantum/Floer cohomology.

Example 1.10
.

(Trivial vector bundles). Given any

closed symplectic

Example 1.11
.

(Hilbert schemes) Given a Riemann surface
\Sigma,
the Hilbert scheme of a trivial bundle from the last example,
Y=\mathrm{Hilb}^{n}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{C})
satisfies our assumptions, with the action coming from weight-1

-action on

\Psi:Y\rightarrow\text{Sym}^{n}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\text{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}

-functorial projection.

Example 1.12

(Higgs moduli)
.

Another important class of examples are various
moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
of Higgs bundles
over a Riemann surface
\Sigma.

Roughly, elements of these spaces are
(conjugacy classes) of stable pairs
(V,\Phi),
where
V
is a vector bundle over
\Sigma
of fixed coprime rank and degree,
and
\Phi\in\text{Hom}(V,V\otimes K_{\Sigma})

is a Higgs field, which potentially can have some poles (here,
K_{\Sigma}
is the canonical bundle of
\Sigma
).
The space
\mathcal{M}
is a
complete
hyperkähler manifold,
with a natural
I
-holomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action given by
t\cdot(V,\Phi)=(V,t\Phi),
whose
S^{1}
-part is
\omega_{I}
-Hamiltonian
with a proper moment map.
It also admits the so-called Hitchin fibration, given as the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field,

(9)

\qquad\qquad\Psi:\mathcal{M}\to B\cong\mathbb{C}^{N}\quad\textrm{ where }N:=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M},

which is a proper surjective
I
-holomorphic map, and it is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant for a certain linear

\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
B.
This makes
(\mathcal{M},\omega_{I})
a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base, with
\Psi
globally defined.

Example 1.13

(Crepant resolutions of quotient singularities)
.

Let
\pi:Y\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
be a crepant resolution,
17
17

17

a non-singular quasi-projective variety
Y
together with a proper, birational morphism
\pi
which is a
biholomorphism away from the singular locus. The crepant condition here is equivalent to
c_{1}(Y)=0
.
where
G\subset SL(n,\mathbb{C})
is a finite subgroup.
The diagonal
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
lifts
18
18

18

by Batyrev
[
Bat99
, Prop.8.2]
, or
[
MR23
, Prop.3.6]
.
to a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
Y
,
so
\pi
is a proper
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant
I
-holomorphic map.
These are much studied in Algebraic Geometry literature due to the generalised McKay Correspondence (see
[
MR23
]
for references).
A subclass of these, when
n=2k
and
G\leq Sp(2k,\mathbb{C})
, give rise to CSRs
Y
(
8.18
), studied in
[
Kal02
,
BK04
]
. Another subclass is when
G
acts freely outside of
0\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, so isolated singularities
\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
, see
[
MR23
]

(here
Y
is a CSR only
19
19

19

see the paragraph before
8.11
for an explanation.
for
n=2
).
The isolatedness condition implies
Y
is convex at infinity, i.e. (
5
) is a symplectic isomorphism with
\Sigma=S^{2n-1}/G
(conjugating
G
so that
G\subset SU(n)
), which was needed to study
Y
using Floer theory.
In our new framework, we can apply Floer theory to all crepant resolutions, without the isolatedness condition, since they are a special case of
1.3
.

Example 1.14

(Torsion submanifolds)
.

Given a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold
Y
over a convex base, and an integer
m\geq 2,
the fixed locus of the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}/m\leq\mathbb{C}^{*}

decomposes into finitely many connected
I
-pseudoholomorphic submanifolds called
torsion submanifolds

Y_{m,\beta}.

They are symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-submanifolds of
Y
over the same convex base, by restricting (
5
) (where we could also replace the restricted
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action by its
m
-th root).
Each
Y_{m,\beta}
contains a subcollection of the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and has strata that converge to different
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.
Looking at one such point of convergence
y_{0}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
,
and viewing
T_{y_{0}}Y
as a complex representation for the linearised
S^{1}
-action,
one obtains the weight decomposition

(10)

T_{y_{0}}Y=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k}.

It follows that only finitely many weights
m
can arise (the weights only depend on the component
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, not the choice of
y_{0}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
),
and that locally near
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
the subbundle
\oplus_{b\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{mb}
parametrises
Y_{m,\beta}
.

1.3.
Floer theory is possible for symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds

Proposition 1.15
.

For symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds over a convex base, a maximum principle holds on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
for the PDEs used in Gromov-Witten theory and in Floer theory, provided one uses the almost complex structure
I
and the Hamiltonian vector field on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
is a positive constant multiple of
X_{S^{1}}
.

The hard part of this Proposition was not the proof, but rather finding the right statement (requiring the existence of the map
\Psi
).
Indeed we can summarise the proof: locally at infinity we have a PDE

(11)

\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-kX_{S^{1}})=0,\vskip-2.27621pt

where
z=s+it
is a holomorphic coordinate for the domain of
u
. As
\Psi_{*}
commutes with
I
and satisfies (
7
), abbreviating
H_{B}:=fR
, the projected curve
v=\Psi(u)
in
B
satisfies

(12)

\partial_{s}v+I(\partial_{t}v-k\,X_{H_{B}})=0.\vskip-2.27621pt

By the extended maximum principle due to
[
Rit16
, App.C2-C4]
,
R\circ v
cannot have a local maximum.

Remark 1.16
.

The same argument holds for the PDE equations used to define Lagrangian Floer cohomology and the
A_{\infty}
-equations for the (wrapped) Fukaya category.
For the purpose of proving a maximum principle as above, one does not require a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action, one just requires (
5
) and
\Psi_{*}X=X_{H_{B}}
for the Hamiltonian vector field
X
that one wishes to use on
Y
.

The moment map
H
in (
1
) plays a role similar to the radial coordinate
R
for symplectic manifolds convex at infinity.
20
20

20

In the case of Liouville manifolds
[
Sei08
]
and of symplectic manifolds convex at infinity
[
Rit10
]
, one works with the class of Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda}:M\to\mathbb{R}
which at infinity are “radial”: they only depend on
R
and they are eventually linear in
R
with a generic slope
\lambda>0
. The chain-level generators of
Hamiltonian Floer cohomology
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
are the
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
. Due to a 1-to-1 correspondence between
T
-periodic Reeb orbits in
\Sigma=\{R=1\}
and
1
-orbits of a radial Hamiltonian arising with slope value
T:=H_{\lambda}^{\prime}(R)
, one has control over those generators. The generic slope
\lambda
ensures that there are no
1
-orbits in the region at infinity where
H_{\lambda}
is linear. The construction is motivated by cotangent bundles
M=T^{*}N
, where the Reeb flow is the geodesic flow for
N
on the sphere bundle
\Sigma\cong STN
; the slope
\lambda
corresponds to the period of the geodesic
[
Vit96
]
; and the limit of the
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
recovers the homology of the free loop space of
N
.

So it is natural to consider Hamiltonians which are functions
c(H)
of
H
, that become linear at infinity. We have control over
1
-orbits arising at slope
c^{\prime}(H)=\lambda
, as follows.

Lemma 1.17
.

By considering the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-flow, and the subgroup
G_{\lambda}:=\langle e^{2\pi i\lambda}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we have

(1\textrm{-orbits of }\lambda H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}(\lambda\textrm{-periodic orbits of the }S^{1}\textrm{-flow})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}(G_{\lambda}\textrm{-fixed points in }Y).

Non-constant
1
-orbits can only arise if
\lambda=\tfrac{k}{m}
is rational, for
k,m
coprime, in which case
G_{\lambda}\cong\mathbb{Z}/m
.
They in fact arise in Morse–Bott families, intersecting the torsion submanifolds
Y_{m,\beta}
from
1.14
:

(13)

B_{\frac{k}{m},\beta}\cong Y_{m,\beta}\cap\{c^{\prime}(H)=\tfrac{k}{m}\},

so only finitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
can arise (here it is understood that we choose
c^{\prime\prime}(H)>0
when
c^{\prime}(H)=\tfrac{k}{m}
).
These are connected odd-dimensional smooth manifolds. Only the
non-compact

Y_{m,\beta}
arise in (
13
), which we call
outer torsion manifolds
, and we call the
c^{\prime}(H)=\tfrac{k}{m}
in (
13
) the
outer
S^{1}
-periods
. Compact torsion manifolds
Y_{m,\beta}
, which lie in
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
, do not arise in (
13
), as we ensure that the only
1
-orbits of
c(H)
near
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
are the constant
1
-orbits at points of
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.
Call
\lambda>0

generic
if it is not an
S^{1}
-period (e.g. irrational
\lambda
). We write
H_{\lambda}:=c(H)
to mean that
c^{\prime}(H)=\lambda
at infinity, for generic
\lambda
, so there are no
1
-orbits at infinity.

So far, it was not essential that
H
in (
1
) is proper, but from now on we will need to assume this – we show in
6.5
that one can tweak
\omega
so that this holds.
We then construct
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
, which at the chain level is generated by the
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
, and we prove the following.

Corollary 1.18
.

There is a well-defined symplectic cohomology algebra
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi):=\varinjlim HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
obtained as a direct limit over continuation maps as the generic slopes
\lambda\to\infty
, admitting a canonical unital algebra homomorphism
c^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
.

Remark 1.19
.

The Corollary holds more generally if the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
is only defined on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
, as it only relies on using (
5
) or (
7
) to achieve a maximum principle at infinity. For example,
Y
can be a convex symplectic manifold with periodic Reeb flow at infinity.
However, in this generality the
Q_{\varphi}
element in
Footnote

4
is no longer defined, and the vanishing result from
1.22
may cease to hold.
21
21

21

E.g. this is the case for open Riemann surfaces
[
Sei08
, Ex.3.3]
.

Remark 1.20
.

Although
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
a priori might depend on the choice of action
\varphi,
we show in

Footnote

52
that at least for commuting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions over an affine variety it actually does not depend on
\varphi
(this includes almost all examples from
Section

1.2
).

Remark 1.21
.

Groman
[
Gro15
]
defines a universal symplectic cohomology for symplectic manifolds
Y
that are geometrically bounded. The philosophical idea is that rather than choosing a specific growth of Hamiltonians at infinity, one would like to use as many Hamiltonians as possible, and take a huge direct limit of Floer cohomologies. A stumbling block for us, however, was that it is not entirely straightforward to check all the conditions required for this construction, partly because the Riemannian geometry of our spaces
(Y,\omega)
at infinity is not well understood. We, therefore, opted to choose a specific class of Hamiltonians, since our spaces
Y
come with a natural choice of
H
via (
1
). This allows us to obtain meaningful filtered invariants that are sensitive to the choice of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.

Proposition 1.22
.

When
c_{1}(Y)=0,
the
\varphi
-symplectic cohomology vanishes:
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0.

To prove this result, we show that the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of generators of

HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
becomes arbitrarily negative
as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
.
This phenomenon is typical for Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-actions, and
generalises previous vanishing results, seen for
\mathbb{C}^{n}

[
Oan04
]
, ALE spaces
[
Rit10
]
, many non-compact symplectic Calabi-Yau manifolds
[
Rit14
]
, and crepant resolutions of isolated quotient singularities
[
MR23
]
.

1.22
may have implications for obstructions to the existence of certain exact Lagrangians in the Liouville manifold
(Y,\omega_{J})
, when
Y
is a
Higgs branch moduli space
, i.e. a hyperkähler reduction of a unitary action on a flat vector space, such as quiver and hypertoric varieties. In the special case of ADE resolutions,

[
Rit10
]
used that vanishing to show that closed exact Lagrangians are 2-spheres. This suggests:

Conjecture 1.23
.

Let
Y
be a Higgs branch moduli space admitting a closed exact Lagrangian
L\subset(Y,\omega_{J})
. Then we conjecture that a deformation argument as in
[
Rit10
, Lem.50]
and
[
BR20
, Cor.1.6]
implies the vanishing of the twisted symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}(Y,\omega_{J},\underline{\Lambda}_{\tau\omega_{I}})=0
.
If this indeed holds, then
22
22

22

The symplectic cohomology twisted by the transgression
\tau(\omega_{I})\in H^{1}(\mathcal{L}Y)
was constructed by Ritter
[
Rit09
]
. By
[
Rit10
, Cor.17]
,
\tau(j^{*}\omega)\neq 0
where
j:L\hookrightarrow Y
(using
\mathrm{char}\,k=2
in Floer theory if
L
is non-orientable). Also,
\tau(j^{*}\omega_{I})
can be identified with a homomorphism
\pi_{2}(L)\to\mathbb{R}
, via
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(L;\mathbb{R})\to\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{2}(L),\mathbb{R})\subset H^{1}(\mathcal{L}Y;\mathbb{R})
, see
[
Rit10
, Sec.4.1]
or
[
Rit09
, Sec.6.1]
.
That vanishing of twisted symplectic cohomology yields a bound on the HZ-capacity is due to Irie
[
Iri14
, 21, Cor.3.5]
and Albers–Frauenfelder–Oancea
[
AFO17
, Sec.3.1–3.2]
.

it follows that

H^{2}(L;\mathbb{R})\neq 0
,
\pi_{2}(L)
is infinite, and the Hofer-Zehnder capacity of
(Y,\omega_{J})
is finite and bounded above by (
16
); in particular there are
no Lagrangian spheres when
dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y>2.

1.4.
Floer theory induces an
\mathbb{R}
-ordered filtration by ideals on quantum cohomology

Theorem 1.24
.
23
23

23

We discuss the field of coefficients
\mathbb{K}
in
7.6
, and mild technical assumptions on
Y
in
6.7
.

There is a filtration by graded ideals of
QH^{*}(Y)
, ordered by
p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,
24
24

24

When
c_{1}(Y)=0,

\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)
for large enough
\lambda,
due to the vanishing from
1.22
.

(14)

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\lambda\geq p}\left(\ker c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\right),\qquad\mathscr{F}_{\infty}^{\varphi}:=QH^{*}(Y),

where
c_{\lambda}^{*}
is a continuation map, a grading-preserving
QH^{*}(Y)
-module homomorphism.

As
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}
are ideals, if
1\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}
then
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)
(“unity is the last to die”).

This filtration is sensitive to the choice of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
(e.g. see Examples
1.34
,
1.44
,

1.49
).

In
[
RŽ24b
]
we prove that the filtration satisfies the following
stability property
:

(15)

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\textrm{ if there are no outer }S^{1}\textrm{-periods in the interval }(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}].

Remark 1.25
.

x\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}(QH^{*}(Y))
means
x
can be represented as a Floer chain involving non-constant
S^{1}
-orbits of period
\leq p.
In
[
RŽ24b
]
we build an explicit map
SH^{*-1}_{(0,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\to QH^{*}(Y)
with image
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
.

In
1.13
,
the cohomological McKay correspondence states that the rank of
H^{2d}(Y)
is the number of conjugacy classes in
G
with “age grading”
d
. However, an explicit correspondence between conjugacy classes and elements of
H^{*}(Y)
was only obtained for certain families of examples, most notably by Kaledin
[
Kal02
]
.
Our filtration sometimes refines the age grading filtration (
8.18
).

In algebraic geometry, and by different methods, Bellamy–Schedler
[
BS18
]
constructed vector-space filtrations on cohomologies of certain CSRs.
For
A_{n}
-singularities, our filtration agrees with theirs degree-wise using a certain
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action (
8.17
), but for other actions we get different filtrations.

Remark 1.26

(Capacities)
.

Our filtration encodes a large collection of invariants in an algebraic way. Among those, the following invariant measures when the unit first appears,
1\in\mathscr{F}_{c(Y,\omega)}^{\varphi}
,

(16)

c(Y,\omega):=\min\{p:\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)\}\in(0,\infty].

This value is finite precisely if
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
, by
Footnote

4
. Such numbers, in various contexts, are referred to as “symplectic capacity” or “spectral invariant”.
The idea of asking what lowest/largest value of an action filtration is needed, for a Floer-theoretic class to appear in an action-filtered subgroup, goes back to Viterbo
[
Vit92
]
,
[
Vit99
, Sec.5.3]
. It appears, among others, in Oh
[
Oh97
]
; Schwarz
[
Sch00
, Sec.2.2]
; for (exact) cotangent bundles in Irie
[
Iri14
, Sec.3]
(with relations to
Hofer–Zehnder capacities, see also Albers–Frauenfelder–Oancea
[
AFO17
, Sec.3.1-3.2]
);
for 4-dimensional Liouville domains in Hutchings’s ECH capacities
[
Hut11
]
, and for
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology for domains in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
in Hutchings–Gutt
[
GH18
, Sec.4]
.

1.5.
Isomorphism invariance

The choice of
\Psi
in (
5
) is auxiliary information: it does not affect
c^{*}_{\lambda}
in (
14
), it is only needed to ensure quantum/ Floer cohomology are defined. An
isomorphism
25
25

25

One may wish to weaken the notion of isomorphism. The standard parametrised moduli space argument, that proves
QH^{*}(Y)
is invariant under deformations of
\omega
or of the almost complex structure
I
, applies in our setting provided that this is accompanied by a deformation of the map
\Psi
in (
5
), satisfying (
6
) or (
7
), so a maximum principle applies. Without that, one needs to justify why parametrised moduli spaces remain compact during the deformation.

of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds
j:Y\to Y^{\prime}
is a pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant symplectomorphism (no conditions on
\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}
).
If
j:Y\to Y^{\prime}
is a pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant diffeomorphism then, after replacing
\omega_{Y}
with
j^{*}\omega_{Y^{\prime}}
on
Y
,
j
becomes an isomorphism of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds.

Proposition 1.27
.

\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}(QH^{*}(Y))

is an invariant of
Y
up to isomorphism.

1.6.
Periodic persistence module, and barcodes

The results of our paper can be rephrased in the modern language of persistence modules. We refer the reader to
[
PRSZ20
]
and references therein, for an overview of this language.
In our setup, we have
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces

V_{0}:=QH^{*}(Y),\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad V_{p}:=HF^{*}(H_{p}),

defined for
0<p\in\{\textrm{generic slopes}\}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
We extend this to non-generic slopes
p
by the
convention

V_{p}:=HF^{*}(H_{p^{+}}),\quad\textrm{ for }p^{+}>p\textrm{ sufficiently close to }p.

It does not matter which
p^{+}
we choose, up to a continuation isomorphism.
We have continuation maps

\psi_{p^{\prime},p}:V_{p}\to V_{p}^{\prime}\qquad\textrm{ for }\;p\leq p^{\prime}\qquad(\textrm{ with }\psi_{p,p}=\mathrm{id}),

with the convention that we replace
p
by
p^{+}
if
p
is not a generic slope, and similarly for
p^{\prime}
.
These maps are compatible:
\psi_{p^{\prime\prime},p^{\prime}}\circ\psi_{p^{\prime},p}=\psi_{p^{\prime\prime},p}
for
p\leq p^{\prime}\leq p^{\prime\prime}.

Extend the definition by

V_{\infty}:=SH^{*}(Y)=\varinjlim V_{p},

taking the direct limit over those directed maps.
We will explain later how we wish to define
V_{p}
for
p<0
, but for now let us temporarily declare
V_{p}:=V_{0}
for
p<0
with
\psi_{p^{\prime},p}=\mathrm{id}
for
p\leq p^{\prime}\leq 0
.

Then we have defined a
persistence module

V
, in the weak sense of a functor

(\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\},\geq)\to\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}

to the category of finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces.

It satisfies
properness
:
\psi_{p^{\prime},p}
is an isomorphism if
p,p^{\prime}
lie in the same connected component of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\textrm{critical slopes}\}
. The
critical slopes
are the outer
S^{1}
-periods of the
S^{1}
-action; they form a closed discrete subset of
[0,\infty)
.
The
spectral points
,
\mathrm{Spec}(V)\subset\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, are the values
p\in\mathbb{R}
which do not admit an open interval around
p
on which the
\psi
-maps are isomorphisms, so in our case

\mathrm{Spec}(V)\subset\{\textrm{critical slopes}\}.

We have a
semi-continuity property
: given
p
, the map
\psi_{p^{\prime},p}
is an isomorphism for any
p^{\prime}>p
sufficiently close to
p
. We differ here slightly from
[
PRSZ20
]
, as our semi-continuity is from above (due to our convention about using
p^{+}
, mentioned earlier).

We do not have the additional finite-type requirement from
[
PRSZ20
]
that
V_{p}=0
for
p\ll 0
, but the above properness condition is almost as good.

In practice, the
V_{p}
only change when
p
hits a critical slope
p
, so one could also consider
V_{p}
just for critical slope values
p_{0}=0<p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots\to\infty
. However, we prefer to keep the parameter
p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, as it encodes non-trivial information about the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action, as we shall see in examples.

The
(p^{\prime},p)
-persistent cohomology
is

P_{p^{\prime},p}:=\mathrm{image}(\psi_{p^{\prime},p})\cong V_{p}/\ker\psi_{p^{\prime},p}.

In particular, much of our paper studies the filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}\subset QH^{*}(Y)
which determines

(17)

P_{p,0}:=\mathrm{image}(c_{p}^{*})\cong HF^{*}(H_{p})/\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}

(using
c_{p^{+}}^{*}
and
H_{p^{+}}
if
p
is not a generic slope).
The
S^{1}
-action
\varphi
gives rise to isomorphisms

\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda-1})[2\mu],

called Seidel isomorphisms, where
\mu
is the Maslov index of
\varphi
. The
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
are compatible with
\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}
:

(18)

\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}\circ\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}=\psi_{\lambda^{\prime}-1,\lambda-1}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}.

See for example the naturality diagram in
Section

1.12
as an instance of this compatibility.

Corollary 1.28
.

The persistence module is
1
-periodic: for
p^{\prime}\geq p\geq 1
,

(19)

\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:V_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V_{p-1}[2\mu]\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:P_{p^{\prime},p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{p^{\prime}-1,p-1}[2\mu].

In view of this, we wish to define
V_{p}
for
p<0
so that it preserves periodicity for all
p\in\mathbb{R}
. So we turn the isomorphisms in (
19
) into a definition of
V_{p}
for
p<0
, and we extend the definition of the
\psi
-maps by requiring that (
18
) and compatibility both hold.
26
26

26

For example,
V_{-0.6}:=V_{0.4}[-2\mu]
and
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:=\mathrm{id}:V_{0.4}\to V_{-0.6}[2\mu]
, and for example
\psi_{-0.6,2.3}:=\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}\circ\psi_{0.4,3.3}
.

It is not difficult to see that the data
(V_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}}
is (non-canonically) isomorphic to a direct sum of
basic persistence modules
. A basic persistence module is a one-dimensional vector space
W=\mathbb{K}

supported
on some interval
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, so:
W_{p}:=W
for
p\in\mathcal{I}
, and
W_{p}:=\{0\}
otherwise, with structure maps
\psi_{p^{\prime},p}=\mathrm{id}:W\to W
for
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
, and
\psi_{p^{\prime},p}=0
otherwise. Such a decomposition is called the
normal form
for
V
.
We then obtain the
barcode
associated to
V
:

(\mathcal{I}_{j},m_{j})

where
\mathcal{I}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
are intervals, and
m_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of basic persistence modules
W
in the decomposition of
V
which are supported precisely on the interval
\mathcal{I}_{j}
.

In our case, the intervals are of the form
[a,b)
or
(-\infty,b)
, for
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. When
Y
is Calabi-Yau, or more generally when
SH^{*}(Y)=0
, the intervals are bounded. The capacity
c(Y,\omega)
from (
16
) is the longest interval length
|\mathcal{I}_{j}|
for which
0\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
, because the unit
1\in QH^{*}(Y)
is the last to die (if it dies) so it persists the longest.

Hamiltonian Floer cohomology is always
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded, but this can often be upgraded to
\mathbb{Z}/2N
or
\mathbb{Z}
depending on assumptions on
Y
; for example
c_{1}(Y)=0
ensures a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading. The continuation maps
\psi_{b^{\prime},b}
preserve this grading. We can refine the barcode by
(\mathcal{I}_{j},m_{j},d_{j})
by making
m_{j}
only count the dimensions of
\mathcal{I}_{j}
-supported summands
W
that live in grading
d_{j}
(we can ensure the
W
above live entirely in some grading). We call this a
graded
barcode.

Corollary 1.29
.

The
\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}
map corresponds to a translation symmetry on the barcode: the number of intervals
[a,b)
equals the number of intervals
[a-1,b-1)
; and analogously for
(-\infty,b)
and
(-\infty,b-1)
. If we grade the barcode, then that translation shifts the grading by
d_{j}\mapsto d_{j}+2\mu
.

We can also define a filtration of
V_{q}
for any
q\in\mathbb{R}
by

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p,q}:=\ker\psi_{p,q}\;\quad\textrm{ where }p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}.

For example,
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}=\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p,0}
is the filtration in (
14
) for
QH^{*}(Y),

and it determines (
17
), and therefore also
P_{p+k,k}\cong P_{p,0}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In general, we have

P_{p,q}\cong V_{p}/\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p,q}\qquad\textrm{ and }1\textrm{-periodicity:}\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}:\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p,q}\cong\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p-1,q-1}.

The case when
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, described in
1.31
, is particularly nice: in that case, all
V_{p}
have the same dimension, and therefore the pictorial representation of the above barcode means that the death of bars is matched by births of bars (in possibly different gradings), since the sum of all
m_{j}
for which
p\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
must equal the dimension of
V_{p}.

We remark that there is a notion of distance between persistence modules, called interleaving distance, which coincides with a notion of distance for barcodes, called bottleneck distance. Thus, the above assigns a notion of
distance
between two
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions on
Y
(we will see in
Section

1.14
that
Y
often admits discrete families of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions). This will be explored in future work.

1.7.
Specialisation to a filtration on
H^{*}(Y)
by cup product ideals

The Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
is a vector space over a chosen base field
\mathbb{B}
(see Remarks
6.10
and
7.6
). As a
\mathbb{K}
-module,
QH^{*}(Y)=H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K})\cong H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})\otimes_{\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{K}
. To induce a filtration on
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
, the naive intersection
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})\cap\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
is not well-behaved: its rank over
\mathbb{B}
is unrelated
27
27

27

E.g.
c_{\lambda}^{*}(x)=x
,
c_{\lambda}^{*}(y)=Tx
, for
x,y\in H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
, then
y-Tx\in\ker c_{\lambda}^{*}
. Over
\mathbb{B}
the map is injective on
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{B}}(x,y)
.
to
\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}}\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
and it does not respect cup product. Instead, we define the
specialised
\varphi
-filtration

{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B},\lambda}}^{\varphi}(H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B}))={\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B},\lambda}}^{\varphi}:=\mathrm{ini}(\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi})\subset H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B}),

where
\mathrm{ini}(x)
is the initial term
28
28

28

For
x\neq 0\in\mathbb{K}
,
x=n_{0}T^{a_{0}}+n_{1}T^{a_{1}}+\cdots
for
n_{j}\in\mathbb{B}
,
n_{0}\neq 0
, where
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots
are an increasing unbounded sequence in
\mathbb{R}
. The
T
-valuation
\mathrm{val}_{T}(x)=a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
defines the “specialisation”

\textstyle\mathrm{ini}(x):=(T^{-\mathrm{val}_{T}(x)}x)|_{T=0}=n_{0}\in\mathbb{B};

and
\mathrm{ini}(0)=0
.
This naturally extends to
\mathrm{ini}:QH^{*}(Y)=H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})\otimes_{\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{K}\to H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
. For

S\subset QH^{*}(Y)
,
\mathrm{ini}(S):=\{\mathrm{ini}(s):s\in S\}
.

n_{0}\in H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
of the formal Laurent “series”
x:=n_{0}T^{a_{0}}+(T^{>a_{0}}\textrm{-terms})
.

Corollary 1.30
.

{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B},\lambda}}^{\varphi}
is a filtration by cup-ideals on
H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{B})
with
\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{B}}\,{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B},\lambda}}^{\varphi}=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}}\,\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}.

1.8.
Effective methods to compute the
\varphi
-filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}

Floer invariants are notoriously difficult to compute. Nevertheless we developed two efficient tools to compute the
\varphi
-filtration, which we compare in an explicit example in
Section

2
: the Springer resolution of the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32}
.

One tool is developed in
[
RŽ24b
]
: a Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence, whose
E_{1}
-page essentially consists of
H^{*}(Y)
together with ordinary cohomology
H^{*}(B_{k/m})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
of the slices from
Equation

13
, i.e. the Morse–Bott manifolds of period-
k/m

S^{1}
-orbits.
This spectral sequence is induced by the period-filtration we construct on Floer chain complexes.
The columns of the spectral sequence are labelled by the slope values
\lambda=k/m
, so the
\varphi
-filtration value of a class in
H^{*}(Y)
is the smallest slope needed so that the columns up to that slope have gathered enough cohomology to kill the given class in the spectral sequence.
This method relies on knowing information about the torsion
m
-submanifolds from
1.14
, and an analysis of the Morse–Bott–Floer indices
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
.

The other tool, developed in this paper, decomposes and computes the continuation map
c_{\lambda}^{*}
from
Footnote

23
for Hamiltonians of type
H_{\lambda}=\lambda H
. This involves knowing information about the fixed loci
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
and analysing certain indices
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
which are a generalisation of the Morse–Bott indices
\mu_{\alpha}
, that are dependent on the slope
\lambda.
The precise definition of these indices in terms of the weight decompositions (
10
), and the study of their properties, is carried out in
Section

5
.

1.9.
Lower bounds for the ranks of
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}

To simplify the discussion, suppose
Y
has no odd degree cohomology. This holds for all CSRs, and all crepant resolutions of quotient singularities.

Theorem 1.31
.

Suppose
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, then (over Novikov field coefficients)

(20)

HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})].

Thus,
using (
2
), the continuation map in (
14
) for generic
\lambda>0
becomes

(21)

c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}]\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})].

Abbreviate the rank of a
\mathbb{K}
-module by
|V|:=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}}\,V.
For generic
\lambda>0
, define

(22)

\delta_{\lambda}^{k}:=|QH^{k}(Y)|-|HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})|=\sum_{\alpha}|H^{k-\mu_{\alpha}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|-|H^{k-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|.

From (
21
) we obtain lower bounds on the ranks of
\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
; it holds also without the above simplification:

Corollary 1.32
.

Assume
c_{1}(Y)=0
. Let
t=\max\{d:H^{d}(Y)\neq 0\}
,
so
0\leq t\leq 2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2
. Then

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}\cap QH^{k}(Y)|&\geq&\displaystyle\max_{0<\lambda<\gamma^{+}}\delta_{\lambda}^{k}\quad\textrm{ and }&|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{N}\cap QH^{k}(Y)|&\geq&|H^{k}(Y)|-|H^{k+2N\mu}(Y)|,\\[5.69054pt]
&&&\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{N}\cap QH^{k}(Y)&=&QH^{k}(Y)\;\textrm{ for }k\geq t+1-2N\mu,\\[5.69054pt]
&&&\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}&=&QH^{*}(Y)\;\textrm{ for }\lambda\geq\lceil\tfrac{t+1}{2\mu}\rceil,\end{array}

where
N\in\mathbb{N}
. If the action
\varphi
is the
m
-th power of a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action, the above also holds for
N\in\tfrac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.

The result for
N
follows from the general one, as
\mu_{N^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\alpha}-2N\mu
(where
N^{+}
means
N+\delta
for small
\delta>0
), thus the continuation map for “
N
full rotations” (compare
Equation

4
) is

c_{N^{+}}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\cong HF^{*}(H_{\delta})\to HF^{*}(H_{N^{+}})\cong QH^{*}(Y)[2N\mu].

1.10.
Computing the
\varphi
-filtration for full rotations: the
Q_{\varphi}
invariant

Ideally one would like to compute the continuation maps
c_{\lambda}^{*}
, but such computations in Floer theory are usually impossible. An exception is the full rotation
c^{*}_{N^{+}}
in (
4
),
which we discuss in
Section

7.5
. Namely,
c_{N^{+}}^{*}
is
N
-fold quantum product by

Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y),

where the Maslov index

\mu>0
of the
S^{1}
-action is easily computable from (
10
) (
Section

5
).
Denoting quantum product by
\star
,

(24)

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{N}=\ker\left(Q_{\varphi}^{\star N}\,\star\,\cdot:QH^{*}(Y)\to QH^{*}(Y)[2N\mu]\right).

In particular, due to stability property (
15
), when
\varphi
is free outside of the fixed locus, this completely determines the filtration, e.g. see Examples
1.38
and
1.40
.

Proposition 1.35
.

If
c_{1}(Y)=0
and the intersection product

and
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=QH^{*}(Y).

If

is Kähler Calabi-Yau or Fano,
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}

0
and

of the normal bundle of
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\subset Y
is non-zero,
34
34

34

here PD stands for the Poincaré–Lefschetz dual of the locally-finite cycle

.
then
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}\neq QH^{*}(Y)
and

Q_{\varphi}=PD[\mathfrak{F}_{\min}]+(T^{>0}\textrm{-terms})\neq 0\in QH^{2\mu}(Y).

Example 1.40

1.11.
Lowest order approximations of continuation maps

We now try to determine the ideals
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}_{\lambda}

42

42

Quantum/Floer solutions are counted with a factor

We preface that continuation maps factorise well: for generic slopes
\lambda<\gamma

\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}

(27)

\displaystyle c_{\gamma}^{*}=\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}\circ c_{\lambda}^{*}

\displaystyle:QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(\lambda H)\to HF^{*}(\gamma H),

(28)

\displaystyle\textrm{If }H^{*}(Y)\textrm{ lies in even degrees, }\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}

\displaystyle:\oplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]\to\oplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\gamma}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})].

Proposition 1.41

Horizontal arrows are continuations, vertical arrows are chain isomorphisms

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{N+\lambda}=\ker c^{*}_{(N+\lambda)^{+}}=\ker\left(c^{*}_{\lambda^{+}}\circ(Q_{\varphi}^{\star N}\star\cdot):QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(\lambda^{+}H)[2N\mu]\right).

1.13.
Existence of periodic orbits, and upper bounds for the ranks of
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}

The Weinstein conjecture states that any closed contact manifold admits a closed Reeb orbit.
For Liouville manifolds
M
, if
c^{*}:H^{*}(M)\to SH^{*}(M)
is not an isomorphism then the Weinstein conjecture holds for the contact hypersurface used to model
M
at infinity, for generic contact forms
[
Vit96
]
(for example, for
M=T^{*}N
this implies the existence of non-constant closed geodesics in
N
). This relies on deformation invariance results for
SH^{*}(M)
(see
[
BR20
]
and references therein).
In our setup, it is beyond our current scope to prove a stronger invariance statement than the basic one in
Section

1.5
.
Nevertheless, it is under the expectation of stronger invariance properties that one is naturally led to ask what cohomological constraints the filtration imposes on the existence of periodic orbits.

By
Section

1.3
,
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
at chain level involves Morse–Bott manifolds
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
of constant orbits and Morse–Bott manifolds
B_{p,\beta}
of non-constant orbits for various
S^{1}
-period values
p<\lambda
. We build a filtration on the chain complex in
Section

9
(with technical details relegated to
[
RŽ24b
]
), so that

A:=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\subset CF^{*}(H_{\gamma})=:A\oplus B

is a subcomplex for any generic slopes
0<\lambda<\gamma
, where
B
is generated by the orbits with period-values in
(\lambda,\gamma)
. On cohomology,
A\subset A\oplus B
induces the continuation
\psi_{\lambda,\mu}:HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
. So
HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
is the homology of the cone of a map
\nu:B\to A[1]
.
Passing from
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
to
HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
, the
B
kills some old classes of
A
and creates some new classes.
If
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, then
\|\ker\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}\|=\tfrac{1}{2}\|H_{*}(B)\|
(abbreviating total ranks by
\|V_{*}\|:=\sum|V_{n}|
), and

(30)

\|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}\|\leq\|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\|+\tfrac{1}{2}\|H_{*}(B)\|.

It is clear however from
[
RŽ24b
]

that the best approach to this problem is the spectral sequence method, rather than investigating the uncomputable
\psi_{\lambda,\mu}
and
c^{*}_{\lambda}
.
When
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, we get
49
49

49

The first inequality follows from the long exact sequence for the cone, the second inequality is a consequence of
[
RŽ24b
]
. If we omit the middle expression, the inequality is a consequence of Poincaré duality and gradings: only odd classes of the
B_{p,\beta}
can kill
H^{*}(Y)
-classes in the spectral sequence.

(31)

\|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}\|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|H_{*}(B)\|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{0<p<\gamma^{+}}\sum_{\beta}\|H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})\|.

Thus the
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
-filtration imposes cohomological constraints on the Morse–Bott manifolds of non-constant
S^{1}
-orbits, and vice-versa in
[
RŽ24b
]
we use the knowledge of the
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})
to find the ranks of
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
.

In
Section

9.9
we show that the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
and their weights give lower bounds for the ranks of
H_{*}(B)
. With the exception of a phenomenon we call “vertical differentials”, it predicts the spectral sequences that we computed (by finding all
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})
) in
[
RŽ24b
]
. We illustrate this in an example in
Section

2.4
.

1.14.
Different
\varphi
-actions and their filtrations

In summary, given any symplectic

), and (

\{\text{contracting }\mathbb{C}^{*}\textrm{-actions on }Y\}\to\{\mathbb{R}_{\infty}\textrm{-ordered filtrations of }QH^{*}(Y)\},\ \ \varphi\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}.

Powers

In

.

50

51
51

51

(

).
So the

Often

\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions
\varphi

on

Algebraically, two filtrations by ideals

of
QH^{*}(Y)
, for ordered indices

K_{ij}=I_{i}\cap J_{j}
of ideals of
QH^{*}(Y)

K_{ij}\subset K_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}

, which leads to a collection of filtrations of

by ideals.
Geometrically, comparing actions is difficult: asking that two

-actions

7

In

]
we show how to compare
d
commuting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions:

Theorem 1.50
.
52
52

52

Subject to mild technical assumptions on

from
6.7
so that Quantum and Floer theory are defined.

Let
X
be an affine variety with an action by an algebraic torus

\mathbb{T}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{d}

-parameter subgroups contains a semigroup
N_{+}
corresponding to contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions on
X
.
53
53

53

See
3.10
. See also the footnote to
1.3
.

Let
\pi:Y\to X
be a
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant projective morphism. Then:

(1)

The corresponding actions
\varphi_{v}
on
Y
for
v\in N_{+}
are contracting, so
Q_{\varphi_{v}}\in QH^{*}(Y)
exists and

is localisation at
Q_{\varphi_{v}}
and is surjective, and
c^{*}(Q_{\varphi_{v}})
is invertible.

(2)

SH^{*}(Y,\varphi_{v})
is independent of

(up to continuation isomorphism).

(3)

\mathscr{F}_{k\lambda}^{\varphi_{v^{\prime}}}\subset\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi_{v}}

for all
\lambda>0
, for an explicit
54
54

54

k(v,v^{\prime}):=\min\{\langle v,w^{j}\rangle/\langle v^{\prime},w^{j}\rangle\}
,
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:N\times N^{\vee}\to\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{*},\mathbb{C}^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is the natural pairing.
constant
k=k(v,v^{\prime})
depending on

.

(4)

Q_{\varphi_{v_{1}}}\star Q_{\varphi_{v_{2}}}=T^{a}Q_{\varphi_{v}}
in
QH^{*}(Y)
whenever

, so
\varphi_{v}=\varphi_{v_{1}}\circ\varphi_{v_{2}}

\mathscr{F}_{k_{1}\lambda}^{\varphi_{v_{1}}}+\mathscr{F}_{k_{2}\lambda}^{\varphi_{v_{2}}}\subset\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi_{v}}

), where
k_{1}=k(v,v_{1})
,

.

The key idea is to apply

\mathbb{T}
-equivariantly
1.3
: the coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[X]=\bigoplus_{w\in N^{\vee}}\mathbb{C}[X]_{w}
is graded by characters
N^{\vee}:=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{*})\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathbb{Z})
, with
\mathbb{C}[X]_{0}=\mathbb{C}
, and a basis of homogeneous generators
f_{w^{j}}\in\mathbb{C}[X]_{w^{j}}
yields a pseudoholomorphic proper map
\Upsilon:Y\to\mathbb{C}^{N},\;\Upsilon=(\Upsilon_{1},\ldots,\Upsilon_{N}):=(f_{w^{1}},\ldots,f_{w^{N}}),

which is
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant (the action on the

\mathbb{C}^{N}
is prescribed by

). This

allows us to control the Floer theory at infinity for the various
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions.

.

(CSRs) A CSR

1.15.
Example: semiprojective toric varieties

We return to semiprojective toric manifolds mentioned in
1.4
,
to give an overview of results from
[
RŽ24c
]
.
On such a manifold
Y=V/\!\!/_{\chi}G
, given as a GIT quotient of
V
via a torus
G
, there is a residual action of the torus
T:=T_{V}/G\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
, where
T_{V}
is a maximal torus in
GL(V)
containing
G.
It has an open dense free orbit.
Then,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions
\varphi_{v}
on
Y
naturally
arise from
1
-parameter subgroups
v\in N:=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{*},{T})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

We prove in
[
RŽ24c
]

that
\varphi_{v}
is
contracting
precisely when
v\in N\cap\mathrm{Int}|\Sigma|
, where
|\Sigma|\subset N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the union of the cones of the fan
\Sigma
of
Y
.
Thus,
Footnote

52
applies for
N_{+}:=N\cap\mathrm{Int}|\Sigma|
.
Analogously to
1.38
, we deduce information about the full rotation levels
of the
\varphi_{v}
-filtration.

For the next theorem, it was crucial to extend
Footnote

52
:

Q_{\varphi_{w}}\in QH^{*}(Y)
is defined also for boundary lattice points
w\in\partial N_{+}
so that
c^{*}Q_{\varphi_{w}}\in SH^{*}(Y,\varphi_{v})
is invertible for
v\in N_{+}
, and we have
Q_{\varphi_{v_{1}}}\star Q_{\varphi_{v_{2}}}=T^{a}Q_{\varphi_{v_{1}+v_{2}}}
for

. Those
w
give non-contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions so
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi_{w})
is not defined. Let
\mu:Y\to\Delta
be the moment map for the
(S^{1})^{n}
-action, the image is the moment polytope
\Delta:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\langle x,e_{i}\rangle\geq\lambda_{i}\}
.
The Landau–Ginzburg superpotential
W=\sum T^{-\lambda_{i}}z^{e_{i}}

defines the Jacobian ring
\mathrm{Jac}(W):=\mathbb{K}[z_{i}^{\pm 1}]/(\partial_{z_{i}}W)
.
57
57

57

z^{e_{i}}:=z_{1}^{e_{i,1}}\cdots z_{n}^{e_{i,n}}
, where
e_{i}=(e_{i,1},...,e_{i,n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
are the edges of the fan, so inward normals to the facets of
\Delta
.

By Batyrev and Givental,
QH^{*}(M)\cong\mathrm{Jac}(W)
for closed Fano toric manifolds
M
.
We prove that
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi_{v})\cong\mathrm{Jac}(W)
for open toric Fano
Y
:

Theorem 1.53
.
58
58

58

A similar result is due to
[
Rit16
]
under restrictive conditions on

: convexity at infinity, i.e.
\Psi
in (

) is a
symplectic isomorphism
; and the natural Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-rotations
\varphi_{i}
around

) for

\varphi_{0}

\varphi_{0}

is ordinary symplectic cohomology.

For any monotone semiprojective toric manifold
Y
, we have explicit presentations in terms of the toric divisors
D_{i}=\{y\in Y:\langle\mu(y),e_{i}\rangle=\lambda_{i}\}\subset Y
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]/\mathcal{J}\,

\displaystyle\cong\,\,QH^{*}(Y),\;\,x_{i}\mapsto\mathrm{PD}[D_{i}],

\displaystyle QH^{*}(Y)[x^{\pm v}]\,\cong\,\mathbb{K}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{r}^{\pm 1}]/\mathcal{J}\,

\displaystyle\,\cong\,SH^{*}(Y,\varphi_{v})\,\cong\,\mathrm{Jac}(W),\;\,x_{i}\mapsto c^{*}(\mathrm{PD}[D_{i}])\mapsto T^{-\lambda_{i}}z^{e_{i}},

where

\mathcal{J}
is the ideal generated by the linear relations and the quantum Stanley–Reisner relations, and

Q_{\varphi_{v}}=x^{v}:=x_{j_{1}}^{v_{1}}x_{j_{2}}^{v_{2}}\cdots x_{j_{n}}^{v_{n}},

for

in a basis
e_{j_{1}},\ldots,e_{j_{n}}
of a maximal cone of the fan
\Sigma
of

.

Remark 1.54
.

Localising at
x^{v}
inverted all
x_{i}
. For non-monotone

, Smith

[

]
described
QH^{*}(Y)

[

.

Example 1.55
.

Y=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}
, with the natural symplectic form, is monotone.
We make the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
act only on the
\mathbb{C}
factor, by weight one. Then

QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]/(x_{1}-x_{2},x_{3},x_{1}x_{2}-T^{2})=\mathbb{K}[x]/(x^{2}-T^{2})
. Thus
QH^{*}(Y)\cong QH^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{1})
,
Q_{\varphi}=x_{3}=0
and
SH^{*}(Y)=QH^{*}(Y)_{x_{3}}=0
. This generalises to
Y=\mathbb{C}P^{m}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
giving a family of examples that have
SH^{*}(Y)=0,
but
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
.
59
59

59

compare with
1.22
.

1.16.
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology

There is an
S^{1}\times S^{1}
-action in Floer theory for
Y
: the
S^{1}
-action on
Y
via
\varphi
and the
S^{1}
-action by loop-reparametrisation.
60
60

60

i.e. acting by translation on the domains of the Hamiltonian
1
-orbits
x=x(t)
that generate Hamiltonian Floer cohomology, with some chosen weight
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
(e^{i\theta}\cdot x)(t)=x(t-b\theta)
. In our current discussion we take
b=1
.

In
[
RŽ24a
]
we discuss these general actions, but here we only mention what happens when we only use loop reparametrisation. In that case, constant orbits give rise to ordinary cohomology rather than equivariant cohomology. In the conventions of
[
MR23
]
,
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology
ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module (not a ring) with a
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module homomorphism

Ec^{*}:H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi),

where
\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong H_{-*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})
, and
u
is the equivariant parameter in degree two. At chain level, each
1
-orbit gives a copy of the
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-module
\mathbb{F}
. When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we have
61
61

61

For the same grading reasons that prove
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
in
1.22
.

ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0.

Theorem 1.56
.

There is an
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
-ordered filtration by graded
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-submodules of
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
,

E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\lambda>p}\ker\left(Ec_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\right),\qquad E\mathscr{F}_{\infty}^{\varphi}:=H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F},

where
Ec_{\lambda}^{*}
is the equivariant continuation map, a grading-preserving
\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]
-linear map.

In general,
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
. If
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees (e.g. CSRs), then
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=H^{*}(Y)\cap E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}.

Remark 1.57
.

The reason
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
may be larger is that the image
c_{\lambda}^{*}(x)
may lie in the
\mathbb{K}
-span of the images of the higher-order maps
\delta_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, involved in the equivariant Floer differential
d=\delta_{0}+\sum u^{j}\delta_{j}
(for example
\delta_{1}=\Delta
induces the BV-operator on
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, see
[
Sei08
]
). In that case,
Ec_{\lambda}^{*}(x)=0
.

We will see in
[
RŽ24b
]
that the
S^{1}
-equivariant spectral sequence for
ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
often collapses on the
E_{1}
-page, making this approach computationally easier, but it comes at the cost of needing to analyse the presence of repeated copies of generators coming from the parameters
u^{-j}
.
We illustrate an explicit example in
Section

2
for a CSR, the Springer resolution of the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32}
.

1.17.
Filtrations in the context of Fukaya categories

We conclude with a brief comment about a categorical version of the filtration.
Let us make the non-trivial
62
62

62

A starting point is to mimic constructions from
[
RS17
]
, by replacing the radial coordinate
R
by the moment map
H
, and using
1.16
to ensure that Floer solutions do not escape to infinity.
assumption that a compact Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi)
and a wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi)
are defined for
Y
.
By
[
RS17
]
, one expects the Hochschild (co)homologies of
\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi)
and
\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi)
to be modules respectively over
QH^{*}(Y)
and
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
. This would imply that
\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi)
(but not necessarily
\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi)
) is homologically trivial
63
63

63

For Liouville manifolds
M
,
SH^{*}(M)=0
implies that there are no closed
exact
Lagrangian submanifolds in
M
. However, for our spaces
Y
,
\omega
is typically
not
exact, so there is no notion of
exact
Lagrangian, and no known obstructions to the existence of arbitrary closed Lagrangians
j:L\hookrightarrow M
(beyond the homological self-intersection,
j_{*}[L]\cdot j_{*}[L]=-\chi(L)
).
when
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
, so we are interested in slope-filtration invariants analogous to the
\ker c^{*}_{\lambda}\subset QH^{*}(Y)
.

For the compact Fukaya category, a filtration by
QH^{*}(Y)
-submodules on Hochschild homology
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y))
arises from the open-closed string map, so considering kernels of the composite
c_{\lambda}^{*}\circ\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y))\to QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda}).

In the wrapped setup, one needs to decide which non-compact Lagrangian submanifolds
L\subset(Y,\omega)
to allow.
64
64

64

for example including the requirement that
L
at infinity is
\nabla H
-invariant (by comparison: in the Liouville manifold setup, one requires
L
to be “conical” at infinity
[
RS17
]
).

For such
L,L^{\prime}
, consider the acceleration map

f^{*}_{\lambda}:HF^{*}(L,L^{\prime})\to HW^{*}(L,L^{\prime};H_{\lambda}),

where the latter module at chain level,
CW^{*}(L,L^{\prime};\lambda H)
, is generated by time
1
-orbits for
H_{\lambda}
from
L
to
L^{\prime}
rather than intersections points
L\cap L^{\prime}
.
By
[
RS17
]
, one expects this to be a
QH^{*}(Y)
-module map, so
\ker f^{*}_{\lambda}
filters
HF^{*}(L,L^{\prime})
in a way analogous to how
\ker c^{*}_{\lambda}
filtered
QH^{*}(Y)
.
As
\varinjlim HW^{*}(L,L^{\prime};H_{\lambda})
is expected to be a module over
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, if
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
then
f_{\lambda}^{*}
vanishes for large enough
\lambda
.

Example 1.58
.

The construction of the
SH^{*}(Y)
-action on
HH_{*}(\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi))
,
at its most basic level, shows that
q\in QH^{*}(Y)
acting by
c_{\lambda}^{*}(q)\in HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
on
HF^{*}(L,L^{\prime})
, with output in
HW^{*}(L,L^{\prime};H_{\lambda})
, agrees with composing
f_{\lambda}^{*}
with the action
q\cdot
on
HF^{*}(L,L^{\prime})
. Thus,
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot HF^{*}(L,L^{\prime})\subset\ker f_{\lambda}^{*}
.

A simple illustration of this, for
Y=\mathbb{C}P^{1}\times\mathbb{C}
as in
1.55
, letting
L=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
and denoting
L_{\lambda}
its image under the time
1
-flow of
H_{\lambda}
:
HF^{*}(L,L):=HF^{*}(L,L_{0^{+}})\cong H^{*}(S^{1})\subset\ker f_{1^{+}}^{*}
, as
c_{1^{+}}^{*}=0
.

To upgrade the above to categorical level, for compact Lagrangians, we would proceed as follows.
Given a Hochschild cycle
\gamma=[y]\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi))
, consider the acceleration functor

f:\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi))\to\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi)).

Suppose
f(y)
is exact (e.g. always if
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi))=0
), so at chain level the bar differential
b(z)=y
for a Hochschild chain
z
. A Hochschild chain is a finite sum of words of type
\underline{x_{n+1}}\otimes x_{n}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}
, for various lengths
n
, where each
x_{i}\in CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};\lambda_{i}H)
lies in a chain complex as described above (and
L_{n+1}=L_{0}
). We may therefore associate to
z
a slope-value
\lambda(z):=\max\sum\lambda_{i}
, maximising over the finitely many words in
z
the total slope value
\sum\lambda_{i}
of each word. We then define a filtration of Hochschild homology by
p\in(0,\infty]
by measuring what slope is needed to witness the triviality of
f(y)
:

\mathcal{F}^{p}:=\bigcap_{\lambda>p}\{[y]\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi)):\exists z\in\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(Y,\varphi))\textrm{ with }f(y)=b(z)\textrm{ and }\lambda(z)\leq\lambda\}.

This filters
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi))
by
QH^{*}(Y)
-submodules,
65
65

65

By inspecting the construction of the chain map
\psi_{q}
that defines the
QH^{*}(Y)
-action on the Hochschild chain complex by
q\in QH^{*}(Y)

[
RS17
, Sec.8]
, one sees that the total slope
\sum\lambda_{i}
of words will not increase. Thus
f(\psi_{q}(y))=\psi_{q}(f(y))=\psi_{q}(b(z))=b(\psi_{q}(z))
and
\lambda(\psi_{q}(z))\leq\lambda(z)\leq\lambda
.
with
p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, letting
\mathcal{F}^{\infty}:=\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{F}(Y,\varphi))
.

2.
Example: the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32}

2.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{S}_{32}

In
[
RŽ24b
]
we will discuss many examples, in particular Slodowy varieties, out of which we will choose one
here for succinct illustration.
The partition
(3,2)
of
n=5
defines a standard nilpotent
5\times 5
Jordan canonical form
e
, with Jordan block sizes
3
and
2
. This determines a standard
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
(e,f,h)
in
\mathfrak{sl}_{5}
, where
h
is a diagonal matrix with eigenvalues
h_{i}
, in our case
2,0,-2,1,-1

[
Ž20
, Sec.5.2.1]
.
66
66

66

There is a typo in that thesis: the diagonal entries of
h_{k}
are
h_{0}^{k},\ldots,h_{k-1}^{k}
starting the numbering at
i=0
.

This determines a Slodowy slice

S_{e}=e+\text{ker}(\text{ad}f)\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{5}.

The nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}=\{5\times 5\textrm{ nilpotent matrices}\}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{5}
determines the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{e}:=S_{e}\cap\mathcal{N}
.
Now consider the Springer resolution
\nu:\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathcal{N}
, whose fibre
\mathcal{B}^{x}
over
x\in\mathcal{N}
consists of all complete flags
F=\{0\subset F_{1}\subset\cdots\subset F_{4}\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}\}
satisfying
xF_{i}\subset F_{i-1}
.
In fact,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\cong T^{*}\mathcal{B}
where
\mathcal{B}
is the complete flag variety for
\mathbb{C}^{5}.

The Springer resolution restricts to a resolution

Y:=\widetilde{S}_{e}=\nu^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{e})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}_{e},

with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{S}_{e}=4
.
It admits the Kazhdan
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action:
t\cdot(x,F)=(t^{2}\mathrm{Ad}(t^{-h})x,t^{-h}F)
, where
t^{-h}
is the diagonal matrix with entries
t^{-h_{i}}
, thus
t^{2}\mathrm{Ad}(t^{-h})x
is explicitly
t^{2+h_{j}-h_{i}}x_{ij}
on the entries
x_{ij}
of
x
.
It turns out that
Y
is a weight-2 CSR, so the Maslov index
\mu=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y=4
.

The ranks of the

Rank considerations, using

|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1/5}\cap H^{4}(Y)|\geq 2,\;\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1/3}\supset H^{4}(Y),\;|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1/3}\cap H^{2}(Y)|\geq 2,\;\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{2/5}\supset H^{2}(Y)\oplus H^{4}(Y),\;\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=H^{*}(Y).

\mathbb{K}F_{min}

.
