-factor.
∎

Remark 7.27
.

​​
121
121

121

This remark proves a result analogous to an observation due to Seidel for closed symplectic manifolds
[
MT06
, Lm.3.1]
, which is at the heart of McDuff-Tolman’s
[
MT06
, Prop.3.3]
, which is analogous to
7.26
in the closed case. Their semi-freeness condition on
\mathfrak{F}_{\max}
is precisely our condition that the weights are
0,1
at
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
(see
7.21
).
The argument in the previous proof shows the following general observation for the class
Q_{\varphi}
in the setting of
7.23
(and also in the setting of the spaces considered in
[
Rit14
,
Rit16
]
), with the convention that the lift
\widetilde{\varphi}
is chosen to fix the constant “cap” at a point of
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
.

Observation.

The only constant sections that can contribute in class
s_{\min}
to
Q_{\varphi}
must lie at
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
(constant sections at other
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\neq\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
would have to contribute in a class
s_{\min}+\gamma
with
\omega(\gamma)>0
), and non-constant sections can only contribute in classes
s_{\min}+\gamma
with
\omega(\gamma)>0
. Thus, when constant sections at
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
are regular,
122
122

122

Otherwise, one must compute the Euler class of the relevant obstruction bundle over
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
.
they yield the
T^{0}
-part to
Q_{\varphi}
, which is
\mathrm{PD}[\mathfrak{F}_{\min}]
.

Proof
. Non-constant sections
u
in class
s=s_{\min}+\gamma
contribute with energy
u^{*}\widetilde{\Omega}>0
(see
[
Rit14
, Lem.30]
) and
u^{*}\widetilde{\Omega}=s^{*}\widetilde{\Omega}=\omega(\gamma)
, so constant sections have
\omega(\gamma)=0
. Finally, for constant sections, the previous proof showed that the constant point lies in
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
whenever
\omega(\gamma)=0.
∎

We mention one setting where higher order
T
correction terms can be excluded.
The virtual complex dimension of the space of sections in class
s=s_{\min}+\gamma
being counted by
Q_{\varphi}
is
123
123

123

see
[
Rit14
, Lem.35]
using
[
Rit14
, Sec.5.1 and Def.25]
.

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\mu(\varphi)+c_{1}(TM)(\gamma).

If
Y
is monotone
124
124

124

see
6.7
, e.g. non-compact Fano
Y
.
then non-constant sections contribute with
c_{1}(TM)(\gamma)>0
, so they sweep a locally finite cycle of dimension
>\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\mu(\varphi)
(whose Poincaré dual contributes to
Q_{\varphi}
with a factor
T^{\omega(\gamma)}
). In the special case
\mu(\varphi)=1
, this implies that those lf-cycles are trivial for dimension reasons, so
Q_{\varphi}=\mathrm{PD}[\mathfrak{F}_{\min}].
E.g. this applies when
\varphi
is one of the natural rotations about toric divisors in non-compact Fano toric manifolds. For closed Fano toric manifolds, this was first observed by McDuff–Tolman
[
MT06
]
which led to their proof of the Batyrev presentation of
QH^{*}(Y)
.

Corollary 7.28
.

For any weight-
1
CSR,
Q_{\varphi}=\mathrm{PD}[\mathfrak{F}_{\min}]+(
terms with
T^{>0})\neq 0\in QH^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y}(Y)
, so

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}\neq H^{*}(Y).

For any weight-
s
CSR with
s\geq 2
,
Q_{\varphi}=0
, so
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=H^{*}(Y).

Proof.

For
s\geq 2
,
Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y)=0
as
2\mu=s\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y>\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
(by
8.9
and
8.4
). Let
Y
be a weight-
1
CSR.
By
7.22
,

\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
is an
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian and
\mu=\mathrm{codim}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
.
As
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
is
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian, its normal and cotangent bundle can be identified, so
e(U_{\min})=-e(\mathfrak{F}_{\min})
, and
e(\mathfrak{F}_{\min})\neq 0
as the Euler characteristic of
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
is non-zero (its cohomology lies in even degree, by
3.22
and
8.4
.).
Thus
Q_{\varphi}\neq 0
by (
67
) and
7.26
.
∎

An alternative proof of
7.28
follows by non-degeneracy of the intersection form
which holds for CSRs (
8.5
).
When the intersection product is trivial, we find that
Q_{\varphi}
vanishes:

Proposition 7.29
.

If
c_{1}(Y)=0
and

\textstyle{H_{2\mu}(Y)\otimes H_{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2\mu}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
intersection
\textstyle{\mathbb{K}}
is trivial,
Q_{\varphi}=0.

Proof.

Viewing
Q_{\varphi}
as a class in
H^{lf}_{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2\mu}(Y)
by Poincaré–Lefschetz duality, it is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear combination of lf-cycles of dimension
2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2\mu
(since
c_{1}(Y)=0
, the Novikov parameter is in degree zero). But those lf-cycles are all compact cycles as (by definition) they arise from evaluation maps on compact moduli spaces
[
Rit14
]
. Indeed the lf-cycles are supported close to
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
as the maximum principle prevents the sections counted by
Q_{\varphi}
from entering the region at infinity where the maximum principle holds.
The assumption on the triviality of the intersection product implies that
Q_{\varphi}
has zero intersection product with
H_{2\mu}(Y)
. On the other hand, the intersection product
H_{2\mu}(Y)\otimes H_{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2\mu}^{lf}(Y)\to\mathbb{K}

is non-degenerate by Poincaré–Lefschetz duality. Therefore
Q_{\varphi}=0
.
∎

There is family of spaces to which
7.29
can be applied. Consider the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g)
of
G
-Higgs bundles of degree
d
over a Riemann surface of genus
g
(e.g. see
[
Hit87
]
),
for
G\in\{GL(n,\mathbb{C}),SL(n,\mathbb{C})\},

and
d\geq 0
coprime to
n
.
Recall these are hyperkähler manifolds satisfying the weight-1 condition
t\cdot\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
for the canonical
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
.

Corollary 7.30
.

Q_{\varphi}=0
for
\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g)
,
so in particular
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}=H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g))
.

Proof.

Let
d:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{M})
. We have
c_{1}(Y)=0
and
2\mu=d
by the same proofs as in
8.2
and
8.9
.
So
Q_{\varphi}=0
follows by
7.29
together with the fact
(due to
[
Hau98
]
for
SL(n,\mathbb{C})
and
to
[
Hei16
]
for
GL(n,\mathbb{C})
)
that
H_{d}(\mathcal{M})\otimes H_{d}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{K}
is trivial (using
2\mu=d=2d-2\mu
).
∎

7.6.
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology

Applying the methods from
[
MR23
]
,
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology
ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is a
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module, with a canonical
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module homomorphism

Ec^{*}:EH^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi).

Here
u
is a degree two formal variable, and at chain level each
1
-orbit contributes a copy of the
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module

\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong H_{-*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})

where we identify
u^{-j}=[\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}]
, and
H^{*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})=\mathbb{K}[u]
acts by the nilpotent cap product action. We recall the notation for locally finite
S^{1}
-equivariant homology,

EH^{*}(Y):=H_{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-*}^{lf,S^{1}}(Y),

which in this case becomes
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
as it arises from constant
1
-orbits, and
S^{1}
is only acting by
S^{1}
-reparametrisation on
1
-orbits (not on the space
Y
).
Footnote

23
becomes:

Theorem 7.31
.

There is an
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
-ordered filtration by graded
\mathbb{K}[u]
-submodules of
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
,

(69)

E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\lambda>p}\left(\ker Ec_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\right),\qquad E\mathscr{F}_{\infty}^{\varphi}:=H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F},

where
Ec_{\lambda}^{*}
is an equivariant continuation map, a grading-preserving
\mathbb{K}[u]
-linear map.

In general,
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
. If
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees (e.g. CSRs), then
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=QH^{*}(Y)\cap E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}.

Proof.

The first part is analogous to the non-equivariant case, so we will just explain the second part. In general, we have the following commutative diagram, where
in:=\mathrm{id}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}u^{0}
is the inclusion of the
u^{0}
-part, yielding an injective left-vertical arrow below,

\textstyle{H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Ec^{*}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{in}
\textstyle{EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{in}
\textstyle{QH^{*}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{*}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})}

Thus
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
.
Now consider the Gysin sequence from
[
MR23
, Sec.4.5]
:

\cdots\longrightarrow HF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle in}}{{\longrightarrow}}EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u\cdot}}{{\longrightarrow}}EHF^{*+2}(H_{\lambda},\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF^{*+1}(H_{\lambda},\varphi)\longrightarrow\cdots

(the connecting map
b
at chain level yields images of higher equivariant differentials
\delta_{j}
,
j\geq 1
).
So

QH^{*}(Y)\cap E\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}=\ker(QH^{*}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{\lambda}^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\longrightarrow HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})/b(EHF^{*+1}(H_{\lambda})).

The analogue of
Equation

20
, when
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, is

(70)

EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})],

since the
S^{1}
-reparametrisation action on the constant orbits in
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is trivial. So (
70
) is a free
\mathbb{F}
-module in even degrees, on which
u\cdot
acts surjectively, so
b=0
and
in:HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is injective. Thus
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
follows. Also
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})=\ker(u\cdot:EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda}))
recovers (
20
).
∎

8.
Filtration on cohomology for Conical Symplectic Resolutions

8.1.
Topological properties of CSRs

We refer to Braden–Proudfoot–Webster
[
BPW16
]
where CSRs were introduced and studied in detail, although these spaces were also previously considered by various authors, most notably Kaledin
[
Kal06
,
Kal08
,
Kal09
]
and Namikawa
[
Nam08
,
Nam11
]
. In particular,
[
BPW16
, Sec.2]
lists large families of examples of CSRs, including Nakajima quiver varieties, and hypertoric varieties.
We recall:

Definition 8.1
.

A
Conical Symplectic Resolution (CSR)
is a projective resolution
125
125

125

Meaning:
\mathfrak{M}
is a smooth variety, and
\pi
is an isomorphism over the smooth locus of
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.

\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
of a normal affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, where
(\mathfrak{M},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
is a holomorphic symplectic manifold and
\pi
is equivariant with respect to
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions on
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
(both denoted by
\varphi
). These actions satisfy:

(1)

The complex symplectic form
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
has a
weight

s\in\mathbb{N}
, so

\varphi_{t}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

(2)

The action
\varphi
contracts
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
to a single fixed point
x_{0}
, so
\forall x\in\mathfrak{M}_{0},\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot x=x_{0}.
Algebraically,
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{0}=\mathbb{C},
where
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}
denotes the
n
-weight space.
126
126

126

Explicitly

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]\mid(t\cdot f)(x)=f(t\cdot x)=t^{n}f(x)\}.

We call
weight-
s
conical actions
such actions
\varphi
.
A CSR may have many conical actions, of possibly different weights
s,

so we emphasise the choice by writing
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
.
We denote the
core
of
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
by

\mathfrak{L}:=\{p\in\mathfrak{M}\mid\lim_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\ni t\rightarrow\infty}t\cdot p\text{ exists}\}
(see
Section

3.2
).

We remark that our normality assumption on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is equivalent
127
127

127

For the proof of this equivalence see
[
Ž22
, Lem.3.16]
.
to the condition that
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the affinisation
map
128
128

128

\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(\mathfrak{M}):=Spec(H^{0}(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{M}})),\ p\mapsto\{f\mid f(p)=0\}.
that is given in the original definition in
[
BPW16
, Sec.2]
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Any CSR
\mathfrak{M}
satisfies
c_{1}(T\mathfrak{M},I)=0,
where
I
is its complex structure.

Proof.

The top exterior power of the complex symplectic form
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
trivialises the canonical bundle
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{top}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}.

Now recall that
c_{1}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{top}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})=c_{1}(T^{*}\mathfrak{M})=-c_{1}(T\mathfrak{M})
.
∎

In the following Theorem, we summarise some well-known facts about CSRs.
129
129

129

Part (
1
) is immediate (for a proof see e.g.
[
Ž22
, Lem.3.15.]
);
(
4
) is due to
[
BPW16
, proof of Prop.2.5]
, see also
3.14
;
(
5
) is due to Kaledin
[
Kal06
, Prop.2.12]
,
and (
2
,
3
) go back to Nakajima
[
Nak94
, Thm.5.8]
,
and one can find the detailed proof in
[
Ž22
, Lem.3.3]
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
be a weight-s CSR.

(1)

Its core is the central fibre,
\mathfrak{L}=\pi^{-1}(0).

(2)

\mathfrak{L}
is an
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-isotropic subvariety (but usually singular).

(3)

When
s=1,

\mathfrak{L}
is a
\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
-equidimensional variety, so
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-Lagrangian (usually singular).

(4)

The inclusion
\mathfrak{L}\subset\mathfrak{M}
is a homotopy equivalence.

(5)

Any fibre
F
of
\pi
has
H^{odd}(F,\mathbb{B})=0,
for any field
\mathbb{B}
of characteristic zero.

Corollary 8.4
.

The cohomology of a weight-s CSR
\mathfrak{M}
over characteristic zero fields
is supported in even degrees, and at most up to
the degree
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}.

Moreover, when
s=1
,
H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})\neq 0
.

We prove the non-degeneracy of the intersection form of any CSR.

Proposition 8.5
.

Any CSR
\mathfrak{M}
has a definite intersection form

H_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})\times H_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}.

Proof.

By
[
dCM05
, Cor.2.1.14]
the intersection product for fibres above the relevant strata
of semismall resolutions is definite over
\mathbb{Q}
coefficients.
Symplectic resolutions are semismall
[
Kal00
, Prop.1.2]
, so this applies to
\mathfrak{L}=\pi^{-1}(0)
.
Finally, the inclusion
\mathfrak{L}=\pi^{-1}(0)\subset\mathfrak{M}
is a homotopy equivalence for any CSR
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0},
(
8.3
(
4
)).
∎

By Kaledin–Verbitsky
[
KV02
, Thm.1.1]
and Namikawa

[
Nam08
]
, it is also known that any CSR
\mathfrak{M}
has a (topologically trivial) deformation whose base is
H^{2}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{C})
and whose generic fibre is an affine algebraic variety. In the latter variety, there are no non-constant
I
-holomorphic spheres
130
130

130

the affine variety embeds into some affine space
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, and there are no non-constant holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
.

so the quantum product is equal to the usual cup product.
As the quantum product is preserved under deformations of the complex structure (where quantum cohomology is defined over the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
over any base field), we deduce:

Proposition 8.6
.

For any CSR, there is a ring isomorphism
QH^{*}(\mathfrak{M})\cong H^{*}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{K})
. ∎

The next lemma describes the basic information on the fixed locus of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on a CSR.

Lemma 8.7
.

Consider a weight-
s
CSR
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi).
We have the following:

(1)

Its fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}:=\mathfrak{M}^{\varphi}
is a smooth subvariety contained in the core
\mathfrak{L}.

(2)

\mathfrak{F}
is a proper
131
131

131

Meaning: compact in the analytic topology.
variety which breaks into finitely many connected components

\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

(3)

Given a fixed point
p\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha},
the induced
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
has a weight decomposition

T_{p}\mathfrak{M}=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k},\ H_{k}:=\{v\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M}\mid t\cdot v=t^{k}v\}.

(4)

The weight
s\in\mathbb{N}
condition
t\cdot\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
ensures that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}

and each
x\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
the following pairing is non-degenerate,

(71)

\qquad\qquad\omega_{\mathbb{C}}:H_{k}\oplus H_{s-k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.\qquad(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{-duality})

(5)

When
s=1
, we have
2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}+\mu_{\alpha}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
and
\#\{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\}=\mathrm{rk}(H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M}))
.

Proof.

The fixed locus of a reductive group action on a smooth variety is smooth;
132
132

132

e.g. see
[
CG97
, Lem.5.11.1]
.
in particular, it applies to
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{M}^{\varphi}.

It is contained in the core, as it is the preimage
\mathfrak{L}=\pi^{-1}(0)
of the only fixed point
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0},
and
\pi
is equivariant. Being closed in a proper variety
\mathfrak{L},

the fixed locus is proper itself, thus indeed breaks into finitely many connected components
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action at a fixed point
p\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
yields a representation
\mathbb{C}^{*}\curvearrowright T_{p}\mathfrak{M},
thus the weight decomposition (
3
) is immediate.
Considering two homogeneous vectors
v_{1}\in H_{k_{1}},

v_{2}\in H_{k_{2}},
we have

\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(v_{1},v_{2})=t^{-s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(t\cdot v_{1},t\cdot v_{2})=t^{-s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(t^{k_{1}}v_{1},t^{k_{2}}v_{2})=t^{k_{1}+k_{2}-s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(v_{1},v_{2}),

thus,
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}(v_{1},v_{2})=0
unless
k_{1}+k_{2}=s.
So (
4
) follows, as
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
is non-degenerate on
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}=\oplus_{k}H_{k}
.

When
s=1,
this duality forces

H_{0}\oplus H_{-}=\oplus_{k\leq 0}H_{k}\cong H_{+},

thus, abbreviating
|V|:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}=|H_{0}|+|H_{-}|+|H_{+}|=2(|H_{0}|+|H_{-}|)=2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}+\mu_{\alpha}.

Thus, by (
37
), the number of generators in
H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})
is the number of
\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Given a CSR
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0},
if
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is non-singular then
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is non-singular,
\pi:\mathfrak{M}_{0}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is a CSR, with a single fixed point
0.
Thus by the Białynicki-Birula decomposition theorem for semiprojective varieties and the fact that CSRs are semiprojective

[
Ž22
, Cor.2.7 and Lem.3.15.]
,
we deduce that
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is an affine bundle over a point, hence an affine space, thus isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
(with some linear action on it).
∎

8.2.
Symplectic structures on a CSR

We now show that any CSR
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
fits into the framework of Section
6
.
In Corollary
8.15
we construct an explicit
I
-compatible
S^{1}
-invariant Calabi-Yau
133
133

133

due to
8.2
.
Kähler structure on
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
, with an exhausting moment map. We remark however that such a structure (without the exhausting condition)
arises more generally whenever we have a holomorphic embedding
\iota:\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow X
into a Kähler manifold
(X,\omega_{X})
, by
S^{1}
-averaging:

(72)

\textstyle\omega_{I}:=\int_{S^{1}}\varphi_{t}^{*}(\iota^{*}\omega_{X})\,dt.

Any CSR
\mathfrak{M}
admits such an embedding, being projective over the affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.
134
134

134

\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
being projective means that there is an embedding
i:\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}P^{n}
for some
n
(
[
Har77
, p.103]
). As
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is affine,
\mathfrak{M}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}
for some
m,
hence we can obtain
\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{C}P^{n}.

Abbreviate by
g(\cdot,\cdot):=\omega_{I}(\cdot,I\cdot)
the induced Riemannian metric (
\omega_{I}
is
I
-compatible).
Since
H^{1}(\mathfrak{M})=0
(
8.4
), it has a moment map
H.

We show that
\mathfrak{M}
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base in
8.12
. Therefore, if
H
is not exhausting, we can modify
\omega_{I}
so that the new moment map
H
is proper using
6.5
, and we know
H
is bounded below by
3.10
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Any weight-
s
CSR
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold with
an
S^{1}
-invariant
I
-compatible Kähler structure
(g,I,\omega_{I}).

The
S^{1}
-action is Hamiltonian with Maslov index

\mu=\frac{1}{2}s\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}.

Proof.

The
S^{1}
-action is symplectic as it preserves
\omega_{I}
. By Corollary
8.4
,
H^{1}(\mathfrak{M})=0,
so the action is Hamiltonian.
The canonical bundle
\Lambda^{\mathrm{top}}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
is trivialised by
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
for
d:=\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
. The weight
s
condition (
\varphi_{t}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
) implies
\varphi_{t}^{*}(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{d})=(\varphi_{t}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}})^{d}=(t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}})^{d}=t^{sd}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, so
\mu=sd
(see
Section

5.1
).
∎

The methods of Section
6

are required, as
(\mathfrak{M},\omega_{I})
is almost never convex at infinity:

Proposition 8.10
.

Suppose that
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is a non-isolated singularity. Then
any
choice of
I
-compatible (real) symplectic form
\omega
on
\mathfrak{M}
is non-exact at infinity.

Proof.

As
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is a symplectic singularity, by
[
Kal06
, Thm.2.3]
there is a finite stratification
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\sqcup_{a\in A}\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{a}
by locally closed smooth strata, where
\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{0}=0.
By assumption, there is at least another non-generic stratum
\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{1}.
As the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is algebraic, it leaves the strata invariant. As the points in
\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{1}
have finite isotropy subgroups, an arbitrary
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit makes the stratum
\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{1}
non-compact. Thus, there is a sequence of points
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}^{1}
that goes to infinity. Their fibres
\pi^{-1}(x_{i})
are
I
-holomorphic, hence
\omega
-symplectic, projective subvarieties in
\mathfrak{M}.
Thus, integrating their (possibly singular) irreducible components with a suitable power of
\omega
gives a positive value (such integration is well-defined, see
[
GH78
, p.60]
).
If
\omega
were exact outside of a compact set
K
, then a power of
\omega
would also be exact in this region.
But any (possibly singular) irreducible component of a fibre
\pi^{-1}(x_{i})
in this region would have a well-defined fundamental class
[
GH78
, p.61]
,
in particular by Stokes’s theorem
[
GH78
, p.60]
integration of any exact form is zero.
Contradiction.
∎

By
8.8
, when
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is not a singular point,
\mathfrak{M}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2n}
and so it is Liouville with vanishing symplectic cohomology (see e.g.
[
Oan04
, Sec.3]
).
Symplectic resolutions
\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
(not just CSRs) for which

0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is an isolated singularity are completely classified. In complex dimension 2, they are the minimal resolutions of Du Val singularities (by
[
Bea00
, Prop.1.3]
and
[
Ish97
, Thm.7.5.1]
); in higher dimensions, they are the cotangent bundles
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n}

[
CMSB02
, Thm.8.3]
.
In the former case, they are convex at infinity and have vanishing symplectic cohomology for
\omega_{I}

[
Rit10
, Lem.42]
. In the latter case, for
n\geq 2
they are not convex at infinity
[
Rit14
, Rmk. in Sec.11.1]
. Thus:

Corollary 8.11
.

A CSR is convex at infinity only if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
for some
n
, or it is a minimal resolution of a Du Val singularity.

8.3.
CSRs are symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds globally defined over a convex base

Proposition 8.12
.

Any CSR
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold globally defined over the convex base
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, in the sense of Definition
6.1
. Indeed, there is a proper
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant holomorphic map

\Psi=\Theta\circ j\circ\pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},

with
\Psi^{-1}(0)=\mathfrak{L}
,
where
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts diagonally on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
by a certain weight
w>0
. The map
\Theta\circ j:\mathfrak{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
is
a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant holomorphic map, which is a local embedding except at
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.

Proof.

By
8.1
,
\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant, and the coordinate ring of the affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the graded ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n},
whose grading prescribes the weight of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action. Fix a choice of homogeneous polynomials
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}
that generate
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]
. As
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{0}=\mathbb{C}
, we may assume that all
f_{i}
are non-constant with positive weights
w_{i}
.
These determine an embedding

j:\mathfrak{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},\qquad p\mapsto(f_{1}(p),\dots,f_{N}(p)),

with
j(0)=0
. Let

w=\text{lcm}(w_{1},\dots,w_{N}).

The holomorphic map
j\circ\pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant for the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
t\cdot(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})=(t^{w_{1}}z_{1},..,t^{w_{N}}z_{N})
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Let
\Theta
be the holomorphic map

\mathbb{C}^{N}\xrightarrow{\Theta}\mathbb{C}^{N},\ \ \Theta(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})=(z_{1}^{w/w_{1}},\dots,z_{N}^{w/w_{N}}).

Then
\Psi
is proper as
\pi,j,\Theta
are proper.
Finally,
\mathfrak{M}
is connected, as it is homotopy equivalent to

\pi^{-1}(0)=\mathfrak{L}
(
8.3
(
1
,
4
)) and the fibres of
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}_{0}
are connected
[
Ž22
, Prop.3.17]
.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

Explicitly,

\Psi=(\pi^{*}(f_{1})^{w/w_{1}},\ldots,\pi^{*}(f_{N})^{w/w_{N}}).

Denoting
\hat{\pi}
the constant
3.1415\ldots
, the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-action
e^{2\hat{\pi}it}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
has Hamiltonian
\hat{\pi}(|z_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2})
, and we denote its pull-back by

(73)

\Phi:=\hat{\pi}\,\Psi^{*}(|z_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2})=\hat{\pi}\sum\pi^{*}(|f_{i}|^{2w/w_{i}}):\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R},

so
\mathfrak{L}=\Phi^{-1}(0)
. The function
\Phi
is typically not related to the moment map
H
on
\mathfrak{M}
in (
1
).

8.4.
An explicit
S^{1}
-invariant Kähler form with exhausting Hamiltonian

Lemma 8.14
.

Given a CSR
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0},
for some integer
M>0
there is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant embedding

\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
using the diagonal action on the target, which is linear on the
{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
-factor.

Proof.

The morphism
\pi
is projective, thus
\pi
factors through a closed immersion and a projection

\mathfrak{M}\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\mathfrak{M}_{0}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}}\mathfrak{M}_{0},

for some integer
n
. Composing that inclusion
\iota
with projection to the second-factor yields

\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\circ\iota:\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}\times\mathfrak{M}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n},

and thus a pull-back bundle
\mathcal{L}=\iota^{*}\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}^{*}(\mathcal{O}(1)).
By
[
Sta22
,
Lem.01VT
]
that bundle is
\pi
-relatively ample, using that
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is affine and
\pi
is of finite type (as
\pi
is projective, by
[
Sta22
,
Lem.01WC
]
it is proper, and hence of finite type by definition).
Recall that a bundle on
\mathfrak{M}
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-linearisable if it admits a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action linear on the fibres which lifts the action on
\mathfrak{M}
.
As
\mathfrak{M}
is normal,
[
Bri15
, Thm.2.14]
ensures that
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-linearisable for some positive integer
k.
The same holds for positive tensor powers of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}
.
As
\mathcal{L}
is
\pi
-relatively ample, so is
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k},
this follows from
[
DG67
, Ch.II, Prop.4.5.6(i)]
. By
[
Sta22
,
Lem.01VU
]
, the quasi-compactness of
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
(being an affine variety) and the finite type property of
\pi
ensure that some positive power
L:=(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k})^{\otimes d}

is
\pi
-relatively very ample. By
[
Sta22
,
Lem.02NP
]
, as
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is affine and
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is of finite type, such an
L
yields an immersion

j:\mathfrak{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{M}\times\mathfrak{M}_{0}

for some integer
M>0
, with

L\cong j^{*}\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{M}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(1).
It remains to prove that one
can construct this immersion to be
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant, where the action on
\mathbb{P}^{M}
is linear.
That this immersion is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-linear follows by construction, by the same argument as in
[
MF82
, Prop.1.7]
(using work of Kambayashi and Sumihiro). We remark that the proof above is essentially the same argument as in
[
MF82
, Prop.1.7]
for the linear algebraic group
G=\mathbb{C}^{*}
, except we are working with schemes that are proper over
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
(where
R
is the coordinate ring of the affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
) rather than working over
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
.
∎

Corollary 8.15
.

Any CSR admits an
S^{1}
-invariant Kähler structure with an exhausting moment map.

Proof.

Combining Lemma
8.14
with
\Theta\circ j
from Proposition
8.12
we obtain a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant morphism

\Pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}_{0}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}.

This morphism is proper, holomorphic,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant (using the rescaled action on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
as in
8.12
), and locally it is a closed topological embedding. Moreover,
\Pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
is the composite of two closed immersions, so it is a closed immersion.
To conclude that
\Pi
is locally a holomorphic embedding, it remains to show that the differential
\Pi_{*}:T\mathfrak{M}\to T\mathbb{C}^{N}\times T{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
at any point
p
is injective (so that the implicit function theorem applies).
This will follow from the surjectivity of the dual map, viewed as the algebro-geometrical map
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}\to\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{n}^{2}
on cotangent spaces.
135
135

135

\mathfrak{n}
is the maximal ideal of functions vanishing at
p
in the coordinate ring for an affine patch
\mathrm{Spec}(A)
around
p\in\mathfrak{M}
;
\mathfrak{m}
are functions vanishing at
\Pi(p)
in the coordinate ring for an affine patch
\mathrm{Spec}(B)
around
\Pi(p)\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
.
As
\Pi
is a closed immersion, we may assume
\Pi^{\#}:B\to A
is surjective. By construction,
(\Pi^{\#})^{-1}(\mathfrak{n})=\mathfrak{m}
, therefore
\Pi^{\#}:\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{n}
is surjective, and thus the induced map
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}\to\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{n}^{2}
is surjective, as required.

The claim follows by pulling back the standard
S^{1}
-invariant Kähler structure
\omega_{Y}
from
Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
via
\Pi
. If
H_{Y}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
is the Hamiltonian generating the
S^{1}
-vector field
X_{S^{1},Y}
on
Y
, then
H=H_{Y}\circ\Pi
is the Hamiltonian on
\mathfrak{M}
generating the
S^{1}
-action.
136
136

136

(\Pi^{*}\omega_{Y})(\cdot,X_{S^{1},\mathfrak{M}})=\omega_{Y}(\Pi_{*}\cdot,\Pi_{*}X_{S^{1},\mathfrak{M}})=\omega_{Y}(\Pi_{*}\cdot,X_{S^{1},Y})=dH_{Y}(\Pi_{*}\cdot)=d(H_{Y}\circ\Pi)(\cdot),

as
\Pi
is
S^{1}
-equivariant.

As
H_{Y}
is the sum of the Hamiltonians on the two factors
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{M}
, and the Hamiltonian on the
\mathbb{C}^{N}
factor grows like a power of the norm on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
H_{Y}
is exhausting.
The properness of
\Pi
and
H_{Y}
imply the properness of
H=H_{Y}\circ\Pi
, and
H
is bounded below since
H_{Y}
is. Thus
H
is exhausting.
∎

8.5.
Implications from Section
6
and
7

By Section
6
,
for generic slopes
\lambda>0
, the only
1
-periodic orbits of
\lambda H
are the constant orbits inside the core
\mathfrak{L}
given by the fixed points
x\in\mathfrak{F}:=\mathfrak{M}^{\varphi}
of the
S^{1}
-action (i.e. the critical locus of
H
).
As
c_{1}(\mathfrak{M})=0
, Proposition
6.13
implies that

SH^{*}(\mathfrak{M},\varphi,\omega_{I})=0.

The fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}
decomposes into connected components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
which are the Morse–Bott submanifolds for
H
.
At
x\in\mathfrak{F}
, the tangent space
T_{x}\mathfrak{M}=\oplus_{k}H_{k}
has a unitary decomposition given by the weight
k
subspaces
H_{k}
for the linearised
S^{1}
-action, where
H_{0}=T_{x}\mathfrak{F}
.
We defined an even integer grading

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathfrak{M}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}-\sum_{k}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(H_{k})\mathbb{W}(\lambda k)

where
\mathbb{W}(\lambda k)=2\lfloor\lambda k\rfloor+1
for
k\neq 0
, and
\mathbb{W}(0)=0
(see
5.4
).

By Proposition
8.6
,
QH^{*}(\mathfrak{M})=H^{*}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{K})
as
\mathbb{K}
-algebras. Thus,
Section

7
and
Equation

14
yields:

Corollary 8.16
.

For any CSR
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
, the
\varphi
-filtration ordered by
p\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\textrm{generic }\lambda>p}\ker(c_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{K})\rightarrow HF^{*}(\lambda H)),

is a filtration on the singular cohomology ring
H^{*}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{K})

by ideals with respect to the cup product.

We suspect that these filtrations, up to
\lambda
-reparametrisation, do not depend on the choice of Kähler form
\omega_{I},
although we will not try to prove it.
137
137

137

For small deformations of the Kähler form, this should follow by the methods developed by Benedetti–Ritter
[
BR20
]
.

The filtrations however do depend on the choice of
\varphi
.

We now abbreviate
H^{*}
to mean cohomology with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
.
The cohomology
H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})
is concentrated only in even degrees by Corollary
8.4
, therefore Corollary
7.11
yields

HF^{*}(F_{\lambda})\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})],

where
H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
lives in even degrees and
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
is even.
Thus the map
c_{\lambda}^{*}
in
8.16
is a grading-preserving
\mathbb{K}
-linear homomorphism between
\mathbb{K}
-modules supported in even degrees:

H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}]\to\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})],

where
\mu_{\alpha}
is the (even) Morse–Bott index of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(and the first isomorphism is (
37
)).

Remark 8.17
.

Comparison with the literature.

There is an interest in filtrations on the cohomology of CSRs in the representation-theoretic literature. Using a certain contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on the resolution of Slodowy varieties (certain CSRs coming from Lie algebras),
Bellamy–Schedler construct filtrations on their cohomologies,
whose corresponding grading can be read-off from the Poincaré polynomial,
given in
[
BS18
, Cor.2.5]
.
138
138

138

More precisely, it is a Poincaré polynomial on
Poisson
homology of the Slodowy variety

S_{\phi}\cap\mathcal{N}
, whose
resolution

has the same cohomology, up to a grading twist. The variable
x
presents (co)homological grading, whereas the
variable
y
comes from their filtration.

In the example of resolutions of type A singularities there is a choice of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
so that
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}

yields the same grading as theirs.
Explicitly,
given the
A_{n}
singularity
XY=Z^{n+1},
this action is given by lifting the action

t\cdot(X,Y,Z)=(t^{n}X,tY,tZ).
For
n=2
this corresponds to action
(c)
in
1.34
.

Remark 8.18
.

Refinement of the McKay correspondence.

For resolutions of Du Val singularities (and possibly of other holomorphic-symplectic quotient singularities
\mathbb{C}^{2n}/\Gamma
), our filtration yields a refinement of the McKay correspondence
[
Rei92
]
which states that a graded basis for the cohomology of the resolution is in graded bijection with the conjugacy classes of the given finite group using the so-called age grading on conjugacy classes. An example of this for the resolution
\mathfrak{M}
of
\mathfrak{M}_{0}:=\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/5)
is shown in
[
RŽ24b
]
(the spectral sequence for
X_{\mathbb{Z}/5}
). The top cohomology has two filtration levels, which correspond to two pairs of orbits, which all have age grading equal to 1 (the orbits are loops in
\mathfrak{M}\setminus\mathfrak{L}
, and those are
labelled naturally by their free homotopy classes, as explained e.g. in
[
MR23
, Eq.(1.4)]
for the case of isolated quotient singularities, and in the subsequent footnote for the general case).
Thus, our filtration makes a distinction between the orbits lying above
[e^{2\pi it/5},0],[0,e^{2\pi it/5}]
and
[e^{4\pi it/5},0],[0,e^{4\pi it/5}],
corresponding to the conjugacy classes
[\varepsilon^{1}],[\varepsilon^{-1}]
and
[\varepsilon^{2}],[\varepsilon^{-2}]
in
\mathbb{Z}/5
(here
\varepsilon
is a primitive
5^{th}
-root of unity).

The McKay correspondence involves crepant resolutions of quotient singularities
\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma
for finite subgroups
\Gamma\subset SL(2,\mathbb{C})
.
For these to arise as CSRs, we need
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{2n}/\Gamma
for
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
as
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
has a Poisson structure. Apart from four exceptional examples,
this has a conical symplectic resolution
\mathfrak{M}

precisely when
\Gamma
is of type
G^{n}\rtimes S_{n}
, where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group and
G\subset SL(2,\mathbb{C})
is a finite subgroup,

[
BS16
]
.
These
\mathfrak{M}
are in fact all quiver varieties of affine ADE type,
[
Kuz07
]
.
These were studied by Kaledin
[
Kal02
]
and Bezrukavnikov–Kaledin
[
BK04
]
.

9.
Filtration on Floer complexes separating the periods of orbits

In this Section,
(Y,\omega,I,\varphi)
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base
B
, satisfying (
6
). Recall that we can always tweak
\omega
by
6.5
to make
H
proper in (
1
), which we assume from now on.

9.1.
Construction of a specific Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}

We have an
S^{1}
-equivariant proper holomorphic map
\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to B=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
. We abusively write

\Psi:Y\rightarrow B

but it is understood that all constructions involving
\Psi
are only defined on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
.

Figure 2.
Graph of
H_{\lambda}

The Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
will be constructed as in Figure
2
in terms of a function
c
of
H
,

H_{\lambda}:=c\circ H.

This ensures that
X_{H_{\lambda}}=c^{\prime}(H)\cdot X_{H}
, so
1
-periodic Hamiltonian orbits of
H_{\lambda}
corresponds precisely to orbits of period
T=c^{\prime}(H)
of the flow of
X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}
. We construct
c:[\min H,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
so that

(1)

c^{\prime}\geq 0
.

(2)

c^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
.

(3)

c^{\prime\prime}(H)>0
whenever
c^{\prime}(H)
is a period of an
S^{1}
-orbit (these will be outer
S^{1}
-periods).

(4)

c(H)=\lambda_{0}H
on some interval, say for
H\in[H_{0}^{\prime\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}],
for
0<\lambda_{0}<\min\{\textrm{positive }S^{1}\textrm{-periods}\}
.

(5)

c(H)=\lambda H
for all sufficiently large
H.

We also assume that
c^{\prime}
is sufficiently small on
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
so that there are no non-constant
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
in
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
since the potential period values
c^{\prime}
are smaller than the minimal positive
S^{1}
-period.
Thus in
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
the only
1
-orbits are constants at points of the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
submanifolds.
More precisely, we may assume

Y^{\mathrm{in}}:=\{H\leq m\}

is a sublevel set, where we choose
m
large enough so that

Y^{\mathrm{in}}\supset H^{-1}(H(\mathrm{Core}(Y))\supset\mathrm{Core}(Y).
We construct
c^{\prime}
to be small on
[\mathrm{min}(H),m]
, and ensure that
c^{\prime}\neq 0
except possibly at

\mathrm{Crit}(H)
, so that
\mathrm{Crit}(H)=\mathrm{Crit}(H_{\lambda})
, and so that
H_{\lambda}
has the same (constant)
1
-orbits as
H
in that region.
In particular, (1) and (2) above are really only needed for
Y^{\mathrm{out}}=\{H\geq m\}
.

When
Y=\mathfrak{M}
is a CSR,
\Psi
is globally defined over
B=\mathbb{C}^{N}
, and
H_{B}=\pi w\|z\|^{2}
is defined everywhere (the
S^{1}
-action has weight
w
, see
8.12
), and one can pick
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{in}}=\{H\leq m\}
to be any sublevel set containing the core
\mathfrak{L}=\Psi^{-1}(0)
.
We recall that
by the rescaling trick in Remark
6.2
we can get rid of the factor of
w
that would appear in (
6
) for CSRs.

Lemma 9.1
.

The level sets
\Phi^{-1}(q)=\Psi^{-1}(\{H_{B}=q\})\subset Y

for
q\geq R_{0}
are closed submanifolds of
Y.

Proof.

d\Phi=dH_{B}\circ\Psi_{*}
is non-zero on
\nabla H=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
as
\Psi_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla H_{B}
(by
3.3
).
∎

We choose
H_{0}^{\prime\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}
so that we have a nesting

\{H\leq H_{0}^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{\Phi\leq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{\Phi\leq R_{1}^{\prime}\}\subset\{H\leq H_{1}^{\prime}\}

for some values
0<R_{0}^{\prime\prime}<R_{1}^{\prime}
.
The nesting condition ensures that
H_{\lambda}=c(H)=\lambda_{0}H+\mathrm{constant}

in a region of
Y
that covers (via
\Psi
) the region in
B
where
R\in L_{0}:=[H_{0}^{\prime\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}]
, called
linearity region
.
That this nesting can be achieved follows from
\Phi
and
H
being proper.

9.2.
Filtration functional on
B

Choose a smooth cut-off function
\phi:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(1)

\phi=0
on
[0,R_{0}^{\prime\prime}]
.

(2)

\phi^{\prime}\geq 0
everywhere.

(3)

\phi_{0}:=\int_{L_{0}}\phi^{\prime}(R)\,dR>0
.

The choice of
\phi
on
R\geq R_{1}^{\prime}
is not so important. Let us choose
\phi=\phi_{0}
to be constant there.
The cut-off function
\phi
defines the exact 2-form
\eta
on
B
,

(74)

\eta:=d(\phi(R)\alpha)=\phi(R)\,d\alpha+\phi^{\prime}(R)\,dR\wedge\alpha,

and an associated
1
-form
\Omega_{\eta}
on the free loop space
\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},B)
, involving the Reeb field
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,

(75)

\Omega_{\eta}:T_{x}\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},x^{*}TB)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \ \xi\mapsto-{\int}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{R}_{B})\,dt.

Define the
filtration functional

F:\mathcal{L}B\to\mathbb{R}
on the free loop space by

F(x):=-\int_{S^{1}}x^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\lambda_{0}\int_{S^{1}}\phi(R(x(t)))\,dt.

Lemma 9.2
.

[
MR23
, Thm.6.2(1)]

F
is a primitive of
\Omega_{\eta}.
That is,
dF(x)(\xi)=\Omega_{\eta}(x)(\xi).
∎

When
\Psi
is not globally defined, a loop
y\in\mathcal{L}Y
may not have a well-defined projection
x=\Psi\circ y
. Nevertheless, it makes sense to talk about
F(x)
and
\Omega_{\eta}|_{x}
because the relevant integrands in the base
B
will vanish near
\Sigma\times\{R_{0}\}
as
\phi=\phi^{\prime}=0
near
R=R_{0}
(also see
[
RŽ24b
]
for a detailed description of how to define “pull-backs” of the functionals to
\mathcal{L}Y
).

9.3.
The filtration inequality for
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})

Theorem 9.3
.

The Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
has a filtration given by the value of
F.
That is, given two
1
-periodic orbits
x_{-},x_{+}
of
H_{\lambda}
and a Floer cylinder for
(H_{\lambda},I)
from
x_{-}
to
x_{+},

(76)

F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+}).

Proof.

The Floer cylinder
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow Y
for
H_{\lambda}
satisfies

\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-X_{H_{\lambda}})=0.

By
Equation

6
,

(77)

\Psi_{*}(X_{H_{\lambda}})=\Psi_{*}(c^{\prime}(H)X_{H})=c^{\prime}(H)\Psi_{*}(X_{H})=c^{\prime}(H)\mathcal{R}_{B},

noting that
c^{\prime}(H)
depends on the original coordinates in
Y
.
Projecting
u
via
\Psi
defines a map

(78)

v:=\Psi\circ u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow B,\ \ \ \partial_{s}v+I_{B}(\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=0

that converges to
y_{-}=\Psi(x_{-})
,
y_{+}=\Psi(x_{+})
at
s=-\infty
,
+\infty,
respectively, where

k(s,t):=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t)))

is a domain-dependent function.
The key observation is that we chose
\phi^{\prime}(R)=0
except on the region
L_{0}
, and over
L_{0}
we chose
k(s,t)=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t)))=\lambda_{0}
.
Thus, using
d\alpha(\cdot,\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
,

(79)

\eta(\cdot,k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=\eta(\cdot,\lambda_{0}\mathcal{R}_{B})

holds everywhere, and it recovers the integrand used in (
75
).
Combining with Lemma
9.2
,

(80)

\displaystyle F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})

\displaystyle=\textstyle-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dF(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)\ ds

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Omega_{\eta}(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)\ ds

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{S^{1}}\eta(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-\lambda_{0}\mathcal{R}_{B})\,dt\,ds

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{S^{1}}\eta(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})\,dt\,ds

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{S^{1}}\eta(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)\,dt\,ds,

where we used (
78
) in the final equality.
Hence, we reduced the problem to the same computation as in the convex setting
[
MR23
, Lem.6.1]
: abbreviating
\rho=R\circ v
,

(81)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\eta(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)&=&\phi(\rho)\cdot d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)+\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\cdot(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)\\
&=&\textrm{positive}\cdot\textrm{positive}+\textrm{positive}\cdot(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v),\end{array}

where “positive” here means “non-negative”.
To estimate the last term, we may assume that
R\geq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
since
\phi^{\prime}=0
otherwise. Thus,
we decompose
\partial_{s}v
according to an orthogonal decomposition of
TB
:

(82)

\partial_{s}v=C\oplus y\mathcal{R}_{B}\oplus zZ\in\xi\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathcal{R}_{B}\oplus\mathbb{R}Z,

where
Z=-I_{B}\mathcal{R}_{B}=R\partial_{R}
is the Liouville vector field and
\xi=\ker\alpha|_{R=1}
. Notice that
\ker\alpha=\xi\oplus\mathbb{R}Z.
Thus:
dR(\partial_{s}v)=Rz
and
\alpha(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=\alpha(I_{B}zZ)=\alpha(z\mathcal{R}_{B})=z
. So,

(83)

(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=dR(\partial_{s}v)\alpha(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)-\alpha(\partial_{s}v)dR(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=Rz^{2}+Ry^{2}\geq 0.

This yields the claim.
∎

Remark 9.4
.

How one deals with the issue of transversality, without ruining the filtration, is a rather tricky matter that will be dealt with in
[
RŽ24b
]
.

9.4.
The
F
-filtration values on
1
-orbits

Corollary 9.5
.

The
F
-filtration values satisfy the following properties:

(1)

F=0
at the constant orbits, so at each point of
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
;

(2)

F(y)=F(\Psi(x))<0
for every non-constant
1
-periodic orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda}
;

(3)

F(y)
only depends on the Reeb period
c^{\prime}(H(x))
of the projected orbit
y
, see (
84
);

(4)

on non-constant orbits, the
F
-filtration is equivalent to filtering by
-H
, or equivalently: filtering by negative
S^{1}
-period values
-c^{\prime}(H)
.

Proof.

Let us calculate the value of the functional
F(y)
explicitly for the projection
y:=\Psi(x(t))
of a 1-periodic orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda}.
If
x
is a fixed point,
y
lies in the region where
\phi=0
so
F(y)=0.
Otherwise,
c^{\prime}(H(x))=:T
is the period of some
S^{1}
-orbit in
Y
(in particular
T>\lambda_{0}
), and
\phi(y)=\phi_{0}
. Thus

(84)

\displaystyle F(y(t))=-\int_{S^{1}}y^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\lambda_{0}\int_{S^{1}}\phi(R(y(t)))\ dt=-\phi(y)T+\lambda_{0}\phi_{0}=(\lambda_{0}-T)\phi_{0}<0.

The drop in filtration value for
1-
orbits
y_{1},y_{2}
arising for successive slopes
T_{1}<T_{2}
is:

(85)

F(y_{1})-F(y_{2})=\phi_{0}(T_{2}-T_{1})>0.

Thus
F(y(t))<0
strictly decreases when
c^{\prime}(H(x))
, hence
H(x),
increases.
∎

9.5.
Period-bracketed symplectic cohomology

By convention,
x_{-}
appears in the output of the chain differential
\partial(x_{+})
if a Floer trajectory
u
flows from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
.
So
\partial
“increases the
F
-filtration”, since
F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})
by

9.3
.
Also
\partial
decreases
H
, and decreases the period
T=c^{\prime}(H)
. Restricting to
1
-orbits with
F\geq A
defines a subcomplex,
CF^{*}_{[A,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})
, and a quotient complex

CF^{*}_{(A,B]}(H_{\lambda}):=CF^{*}_{[B,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})/CF^{*}_{[A,\infty)}(H_{\lambda}).

These fit into a short exact sequence, which induces a long exact sequence on cohomology,

(86)

\cdots\to HF^{*}_{[A,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*}_{[B,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*}_{(A,B]}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*+1}_{[A,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\to\cdots

Lemma 9.6
.

A Floer continuation map
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
for
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
, for a homotopy
H_{s}:=c_{s}\circ H
, respects the filtration if
H_{\lambda}
,
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
,
H_{s}
are linear in
H
over
L_{0}
, and

\partial_{s}c_{s}^{\prime}\leq 0
over
L_{0}
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\rho=R(v(s,t))
,
and
\lambda_{0,s}:=c_{s}^{\prime}
on
L_{0}
.
We have
\phi^{\prime}(\rho)k(s,t)=\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\lambda_{0,s}
everywhere.
We now use an
s
-dependent
filtration and
s
-dependent filtration one-form,

F_{s}(x):=-\int_{S^{1}}x^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\lambda_{0,s}\int_{S^{1}}\phi(R(x(t)))\,dt,\qquad\Omega_{\eta}(\xi):=-\int_{S^{1}}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x-\lambda_{0,s}\mathcal{R}_{B})\,dt.

Equation

79
becomes
\eta(\cdot,k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=\eta(\cdot,\lambda_{0,s}\mathcal{R}_{B})
.
However, in (
80
) a new term appears because

\partial_{s}(F_{s}\circ u)=d_{u}F_{s}\cdot\partial_{s}u+(\partial_{s}F_{s})\circ u
, but it has the sign needed for the argument to work because:

(\partial_{s}F_{s})(u)=(\partial_{s}\lambda_{0,s})\cdot\int_{S^{1}}\phi(R(u))\,dt\leq 0,

using
\phi\geq 0
and the assumption
\partial_{s}\lambda_{0,s}\leq 0
(see
[
MR23
, Sec.6.5]
for the proof in the convex setting).
∎

9.6
implies that continuation maps
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
can be built for
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
in a way that preserves the filtration (see
[
RŽ24b
]
for details).
Thus, taking direct limits as
\lambda\to\infty
in (
86
),

\cdots\to SH^{*}_{[A,\infty)}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{[B,\infty)}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{(A,B]}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*+1}_{[A,\infty)}(Y,\varphi)\to\cdots

9.6.
Positive symplectic cohomology

Definition 9.7
.

Abbreviate
CF_{0}^{*}(H_{\lambda}):=CF^{*}_{[0,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\subset CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
the subcomplex generated by the fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(the constant orbits have filtration value zero).
The
positive Floer cohomology

HF_{+}^{*}(H_{\lambda})=H_{*}(CF_{+}(H_{\lambda}))
is the homology of the
quotient complex
CF_{+}(H_{\lambda}):=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})/CF_{0}^{*}(H_{\lambda})
.
The direct limit over continuation maps is
positive symplectic cohomology
,

SH_{+}(Y,\varphi,\omega):=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}HF_{+}^{*}(H_{\lambda}).

Remark 9.8
.

When (
7
) holds, the construction of the
F
-filtration is not possible. However one can still define, somewhat unsatisfactorily,
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi):=\,
Cone
(c^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi))
.

Proposition 9.9
.

For any symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold satisfying (
5
)-(
6
),
there is a long exact sequence

\cdots\to QH^{*}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\to QH^{*+1}(Y)\to\cdots

Proof.

The condition
F\geq 0
imposed on generators of
CF^{*}_{0}(H_{\lambda})
means that the generators are precisely the fixed points in
\mathfrak{F}
, and that no Floer solution
u
(with ends on
\mathfrak{F}
) can have
v=\Psi\circ u
exit the region
R\leq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, otherwise it would enter the region
\phi^{\prime}>0
(where also
\phi>0
) causing (
81
) to be strictly positive unless
v
is
s
-independent.
Denote by
H_{\lambda_{0}}
the Hamiltonian of slope
\lambda_{0}
obtained by modifying
H_{\lambda}
to be linear in
H
of slope
\lambda_{0}
for
H\geq H_{0}^{\prime\prime}
.
Then the complexes
CF_{0}^{*}(H_{\lambda})=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{0}})
are equal as the Floer differentials count the same solutions, so their cohomology equals and is isomorphic to
QH^{*}(Y)
by Proposition
7.1
(as
\lambda_{0}
is smaller than any non-zero
S^{1}
-period).
∎

9.7.
Compatibility of the
F
-filtration with the product

We assume the reader has a familiarity with the construction of the pair-of-pants product on Floer cohomology (e.g.
[
Rit13
]
and
[
AS10
, Sec.3.2-3.3]
).
The product on
SH^{*}
is obtained by taking a direct limit of certain
\mathbb{K}
-linear pair-of-pants product maps
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\otimes CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}})
for
\lambda^{\prime\prime}\geq\lambda+\lambda^{\prime}
, and up to quasi-isomorphism this is independent of the choices made in the construction. One does not need these maps for all such choices of
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}
, it suffices to have these for three cofinal families of such slopes, see
[
Rit13
]
.
We want to show that choices can be made so that this construction is compatible with the
F
-filtration.
By
[
MR23
, Sec.6.5]
the
F
-filtration can be made consistent with continuation cylinders for monotone homotopies
H_{s}
. So we can use two continuation cylinders associated to two monotone homotopies to change
H_{\lambda},H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
until they both equal some Hamiltonian
H_{\mu}
, while preserving filtrations. By composing/gluing with these continuation cylinders, it therefore suffices to build, for a cofinal family of
H_{\mu}
, a pair-of-pants map
CF^{*}(H_{\mu})\otimes CF^{*}(H_{\mu})\to CF^{*}(2H_{\mu})
in a way that is compatible with the
F
-filtration.
139
139

139

we may also glue/compose with a Floer continuation isomorphism
CF^{*}(2H_{\mu})\to CF^{*}(H_{2\mu})
on the output.

We will use the model of the pair-of-pants
P
described by Abbondandolo–Schwarz in
[
AS10
, Sec.3.2]
, which admits a holomorphic
2:1
branched covering
P\to\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
of the cylinder. One can think of
P=P_{-}\cup P_{+,1}\cup P_{+,2}
as the union of three half-cylinders such that the positive
boundary of
P_{-}
is the figure eight-loop consisting of the two negative boundaries of
P_{+,1}
,
P_{+,2}
. Under the covering,
P_{-}
covers
(-\infty,0]\times S^{1}
twice, whereas each
P_{+,i}
covers
[0,\infty)\times S^{1}
once. As explained in
[
AS10
, Sec.3.2]
, the Floer equation for
u:P\to Y
corresponds to the usual equation
\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-X_{H_{\mu}})=0
on the three half-infinite cylinders except that on
P_{-}
the time coordinate is parameterised by
\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}
rather than by
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, so it can be turned into
\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-X_{2H_{\mu}})=0
by reparametrising time. We remark that the first term in the integral of the filtration
1
-form
\int\Omega_{\eta}(u)(\partial_{s}u)\,ds=-\int\!\int\eta(\partial_{s}u,\partial_{t}u-X_{h})\,dt\wedge ds
over a cylinder is invariant under conformal rescaling of
z=s+it
. The second term is not, but it will be invariant under simultaneous rescaling of
(s,t)
if we rescale
h
. So for
P_{-}
we either use
F=F_{h}
as defined for
h
but using time interval
t\in[0,2]
, or we use
F=F_{2h}
as defined for
2h
with
t\in[0,1]
.

Lemma 9.10
.

The
F
-filtration is respected by the pair-of-pants Floer solutions.

Proof.

Let
x_{-},x_{+,1},x_{+,2}
be the asymptotic orbits at the ends of
u
, and denote
u_{-},u_{+,1},u_{+,2}
the restriction of
u:P\to Y
to the three half-infinite cylinders
P_{-}
,
P_{+,1}
,
P_{+,2}
.
Integrating the filtration
1
-form over the projection
v=\Psi\circ u:P\to B
reduces to computing three separate integrals for Floer solutions
u_{-}
,
u_{+,1}
and
u_{+,2}
for Hamiltonians
2H_{\mu},

H_{\mu}
and
H_{\mu}
respectively, over three half-infinite cylinders.
Those three integrals are non-positive by
Equation

81
, so

F(x_{-})-F(u_{-}(0,\cdot))\geq 0,\quad F(u_{+,1}(0,\cdot))-F(x_{+,1})\geq 0,\quad F(u_{+,2}(0,\cdot))-F(x_{+,2})\geq 0.

Considering what happens along the figure-eight in the middle of the pair-of-pants:

F(u_{-}(0,\cdot))=F(u_{+,1}(0,\cdot))+F(u_{+,2}(0,\cdot)).

Combining the two equations yields the required inequality

F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+,1})+F(x_{+,2})
.
∎

9.8.
Simplification of the construction when
H
is a function of
R

Suppose that on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
the moment map
H
can be written as a function of
R\circ\Psi
,

(87)

H=\rho(R\circ\Psi),

for some function
\rho:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to\mathbb{R}
. This does not often appear to hold, since
\|X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|
is typically not constant on a level set of
H
. However, it holds for

T^{*}X
for a projective variety
X
(
Section

B.1
) and for
negative vector bundles
[
Rit14
, Sec.11.2]
.
In this case, one can considerably simplify the construction because level sets of
H
map into level sets of
R.

Note
\rho
is strictly increasing as
\Psi_{*}\nabla H=\Psi_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+},B}=\nabla R
.
In this setup, it suffices that
c(H)
satisfies conditions (1)-(3) in
Section

9.1
. Define

\textstyle h(R):=\int_{0}^{R}c^{\prime}(\rho(r))\,dr,

so
h^{\prime}(R)=c^{\prime}(\rho(R))
, and redefine the filtration functional
F:\mathcal{L}B\to\mathbb{R}
as

\textstyle F(x):=-\int_{S^{1}}x^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\lambda_{0}\int_{S^{1}}\int_{0}^{R(x(t))}\phi^{\prime}(\tau)h^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau\,dt.

Also, redefine the
1
-form
\Omega_{\eta}
on
\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},B)
using
X_{h}=h^{\prime}(R)\mathcal{R}_{B}
:

(88)

\textstyle\Omega_{\eta}:T_{x}\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},x^{*}TB)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \ \xi\mapsto-{\int}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x-X_{h})\,dt.

By
[
MR23
, Thm.6.2(1)]
, we again have
dF(x)(\xi)=\Omega_{\eta}(x)(\xi).
Abbreviating
v=v(s,t)
, and using
k(s,t)=c^{\prime}(H\circ u)=c^{\prime}(\rho(R(v)))=h^{\prime}(R(v)),

(
79
) becomes

(89)

\eta(\cdot,k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=\eta(\cdot,X_{h}),

irrespective of our choice of
\phi
. Thus, we can pick
\phi
to just satisfy the two conditions:
\phi=0
for
R\leq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\phi^{\prime}>0
for
R>R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
(similarly to
[
MR23
, Sec.6.3]
). This construction (when (
87
) holds) also applies in the more complicated filtration setting of
[
RŽ24b
]
, where a very complicated
\phi
-function would otherwise need to be constructed.

9.5
still holds, but the proof needs to be slightly modified: in (
84
) we have
F(y)=\chi(R(y))
where

\textstyle\chi(R):=-\phi(R)h^{\prime}(R)+\int_{0}^{R}\phi^{\prime}(\tau)h^{\prime}(\tau)d\tau,

and
\chi^{\prime}(R)=-\phi(R)h^{\prime\prime}(R)\leq 0
, so
\chi
strictly decreases when evaluated at
R
-values of projected
1
-orbits.
In addition, (
81
)/(
83
) imply that
F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})<0
unless
\partial_{s}v\equiv 0
, or
v
lies in the region
R\leq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
.

9.9.
Rank estimates

We now justify the statements in
Section

1.13
.
Fix a choice of generic slopes
0<\lambda<\gamma
.
Let
A:=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
. By construction,
CF^{*}(H_{\gamma})=A\oplus B
, where
B
is the linear subspace spanned by
1
-orbits of
H_{\gamma}
that are not
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
(these correspond to
S^{1}
-orbits with period values
p\in(\lambda,\gamma)
).
By
9.5
.(
4
), there is a filtration value
F_{\lambda}
such that
1
-orbits in
A
satisfy
F\geq F_{\lambda}
. Similarly, there is a filtation value
F_{\gamma}
such that the
1
-orbits in
B
satisfy
F\in[F_{\gamma},F_{\lambda})
.
By
Section

9.5
,
A
is a subcomplex of
A\oplus B
. Thus, the Floer differential on
A\oplus B
has the form

d_{\gamma}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{\lambda}&\nu\\
0&d_{B}\end{smallmatrix}\right):A\oplus B\to(A\oplus B)[1].

In particular,
H^{*}(A,d_{\lambda})\cong HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
, which is
QH^{*}(M)
for small
\lambda>0
. Also, by construction, the inclusion
A\to A\oplus B
induces the continuation
\psi_{\lambda,\mu}:HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
.

Remark 9.11
.

Although we do not use this here,
in
[
RŽ24b
]
we construct the Morse–Bott–Floer model for
CF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
consisting of the critical points of auxiliary Morse functions on the Morse–Bott manifolds

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(of period
p:=0
) and
B_{p,\beta}
(for period values
p>0
). In particular,
B
is generated by the
critical points in
B_{p,\beta}
for period values
p\in(\lambda,\gamma)
.
By
[
RŽ24b
]
, in each degree
k
the following bounds hold

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda<p<\gamma}\sum_{\beta}|H_{k}(B_{p,\beta})|\geq|H_{k}(B)|.

Lemma 9.12
.

HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
is the homology of the cone of
\nu:B\to A[1]
, yielding the LES
140
140

140

Remark. For
\lambda=0^{+}
,
H_{*}(B,d_{B})=:HF^{*}_{+}(H_{\gamma})
, whose direct limit is
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
as
\gamma\to\infty
; and the long exact sequence becomes
\cdots\to SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\to QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\to\cdots
.

\cdots{\longrightarrow}H_{*-1}(B,d_{B})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{*-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{project}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{*}(B,d_{B})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots

thus

HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})\cong\ker\nu_{*}\oplus\tfrac{HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})}{\mathrm{im}\,\nu_{*-1}}

as
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces.

Proof.

In the notation of
Section

9.5
, the subcomplex
CF^{*}_{[F_{\lambda},\infty)}(H_{\gamma})\cong A
of
CF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
defines the quotient complex
CF^{*}_{[F_{\gamma},F_{\lambda})}(H_{\gamma})\cong B
. The long exact sequence (
86
) is precisely the LES associated to the cone of the inclusion
A\subset A\oplus B
. We now exploit exactness and rank-nullity. The first claim follows from the general construction of cones:
HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})\cong\mathrm{im}\,(\textrm{project})\oplus\ker(\textrm{project})
, then use the LES, in particular
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})/\ker\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}\cong\mathrm{im}\,\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}
and
\ker\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}=\mathrm{im}\,\nu_{*-1}
. The second claim follows.
∎

Remark 9.13
.

Cycles
(a,b)\in A\oplus B
satisfy
d_{B}(b)=0
and
d_{\lambda}(a)=-\nu(b)
(and
d_{\lambda}\circ\nu=-\nu\circ d_{B}
due to
d^{2}=0
).
By the above LES, we identify
\mathrm{coker}\,\psi_{p^{-},p^{+}}\cong\ker\nu_{*}=\{[b]\in H_{*}(B):\nu(b)\textrm{ is exact in }A\}
, and we call these classes of
H_{*}(B)
the
new classes
of
H_{*}(A\oplus B)
.
We refer to
H_{*}(A)
as the
old classes
. The kernel of the continuation
H_{*}(A)\to H_{*}(A\oplus B)
corresponds precisely to the old classes
killed
by
H_{*}(B)
via
\nu_{*}
. Finally,
H_{*}(B)

creates
new classes in
H_{*}(A\oplus B)
via
\ker\nu_{*}
.

Suppose
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees.
Recall the notation (
22
),
which relies on
7.11
,

(90)

\delta_{\lambda}^{k}:=|QH^{k}(Y)|-|HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})|=\sum_{\alpha}|H^{k-\mu_{\alpha}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|-|H^{k-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|.

We now show that
B
kills
|H_{2k-1}(B)|
independent old classes, and
B
creates
|H_{2k}(B)|
independent new classes, and the difference of these numbers is determined by the indices (
90
).

Corollary 9.14
.

Suppose
c_{1}(Y)=0
, and
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees.
Then
\nu_{2k-1}
is injective, and

HF^{2k}(H_{\gamma})=H_{2k}(A\oplus B)\cong H_{2k}(B)\oplus\frac{H_{2k}(A)}{\nu_{2k-1}(H_{2k-1}(B))}

as
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces.
Moreover, the fixed loci
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
and their weights combinatorially determine

(91)

\delta_{\lambda}^{2k}-\delta_{\gamma}^{2k}=|H^{2k}(H_{\gamma})|-|HF^{2k}(H_{\lambda})|=|H_{2k}(B)|-|H_{2k-1}(B)|.

When
\lambda=0^{+}
, then
H_{*}(B)=HF^{*}_{+}(H_{\gamma})
by definition, and

\delta_{\gamma}^{2k}=|H_{2k-1}(B)|-|H_{2k}(B)|\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)=\nu_{2k-1}(H_{2k-1}(B)).

When
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
, the results above hold after replacing
(2k,2k-1)
by
(\mathrm{even,odd})
.

Remark 9.15
.

If we denote the total ranks by
\|V_{*}\|:=\sum|V_{n}|
, then it also follows that
\|H_{\mathrm{odd}}(B)\|=\|H_{\mathrm{even}}(B)\|
, and
141
141

141

This follows from

\|HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\|=\|HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})\|
(using
7.11
).

\|\ker\psi_{\gamma,\lambda}\|=\tfrac{1}{2}\|H_{*}(B,d_{B})\|
(this also holds when
c_{1}(Y)
is non-zero).

Proof.

The first claim follows from
9.12
, using that
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
and
HF^{*}(H_{\gamma})
lie in even degrees, by
7.11
.
The first equality in (
91
) follows by the definition (
22
), and the second equality expresses the difference
|H^{2k}(A\oplus B)|-|H^{2k}(A)|
via the first claim.
The final claim follows because
c_{0}^{+}:QH^{*}(Y)\cong HF^{*}(H_{0^{+}})
, so
\delta_{0^{+}}^{2k}=0
, and
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}=\ker\psi_{\gamma,0^{+}}
.
∎

Let
P
be a
critical slope
: some
B_{P,\beta}
in (
13
) exists.
Let
B
be the complex obtained above for
\lambda=0^{+}
and
\gamma=P^{+}
.
Plot the points
(p,\delta_{p^{+}}^{k})
in the
xy
-plane, for critical periods
p\in[0,P]
(e.g.
(0,0)
at
p=0
). Join the points by straight line segments.
Define
\Delta_{+}^{k}
to be the peak
y
-value of the points. To define
\Delta_{-}^{k}
, we look at the segments along which the
y
-value drops, and add up these drops. Thus:

\Delta_{+}^{k}:=\!\!\displaystyle\max_{\{0<\lambda<P^{+}:\;\delta_{\lambda}^{k}>0\}}\!\delta_{\lambda}^{k},\qquad\quad\Delta_{-}^{k}:=\!\!\!\!\!\!\displaystyle\sum_{\{0<p\leq P:\;\delta_{p^{-}}^{k}-\delta_{p^{+}}^{k}>0\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\delta_{p^{-}}^{k}-\delta_{p^{+}}^{k}),\quad\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\quad K^{k}:=\Delta_{-}^{k}+\delta_{\gamma}^{k}.

We see
142
142

142

Loosely: in the above plot, starting from
\delta_{\gamma}^{k}
one can “climb back up to” the maximal
\delta
-value using drop-segments.
that
K^{k}\geq\Delta_{+}^{k}
. Observe that
\Delta_{+}^{k}
is the lower bound for the number of independent classes of
QH^{k}(Y)
killed by
B
obtained in
1.32
. By definition
\Delta_{-}^{k}
adds up all
positive jumps

\sum_{\alpha}|H^{k-\mu_{p^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|-|H^{k-\mu_{p^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})|
, which force
B
to create new classes in view of (
90
).

Theorem 9.16
.

Assume
c_{1}(Y)=0
and that
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees. Then,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|H_{2k-1}(B)|&\geq&K^{2k}\geq\Delta_{+}^{2k},\\[5.69054pt]
|H_{2k}(B)|&\geq&\Delta_{-}^{2k},\\[5.69054pt]
|H_{2k-1}(B)|&=&|HF_{+}^{2k-1}(H_{\gamma})|\geq|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)|\geq\Delta_{+}^{2k}.\end{array}

The last two inequalities are equalities for large
\gamma
:
HF^{2k-1}_{+}(H_{\gamma})\cong SH^{2k-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\cong QH^{2k}(Y).

More precisely, there is an integer
r^{2k}
depending on
P
, with

(92)

|H_{2k-1}(B)|=K^{2k}+r^{2k}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad|H_{2k}(B)|=\Delta_{-}^{2k}+r^{2k},

such that

0\leq r^{2k}\leq|QH^{2k}(Y)|-\delta_{P^{-}}^{2k}-K^{2k}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{P^{-}}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)|\geq r^{2k}+\delta_{P^{-}}^{2k}.

Proof.

We first prove (
92
).
Recall
\Delta_{-}^{2k}
is a lower bound for the number of independent classes in degree
2k
that were inductively created for slopes up to
\gamma
to ensure that, at each previous slope value
\lambda<\gamma
, the rank of
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
agrees with (
20
).
Thus, the actual number of created classes in degree
2k
is
\Delta_{-}^{2k}+r^{2k}
for some
r^{2k}\geq 0
(compare with the
r
in
Section

2.4
). At each step, the relevant injective map
\nu_{2k-1}
kills classes,
143
143

143

no odd classes of
B
are allowed to survive at any step of the inductive argument (induction on the critical slope
P
), since
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
lies in even degrees; similarly no even classes can kill earlier classes as those are also in even degree.
and the total surviving classes in
H^{2k}(A\oplus B)
is therefore

|H^{2k}(Y)|+\Delta_{-}^{2k}+r^{2k}-|H_{2k-1}(B)|=|H^{2k}(A\oplus B)|=|H^{2k}(Y)|-\delta_{\gamma}^{2k}.

The first equality in (
92
) follows, the second follows from
9.14
as
|H_{2k}(B)|=|H_{2k-1}(B)|-\delta_{\gamma}^{2k}
.
For the bound on
r^{2k}
, note that

H_{2k-1}(B)
maps injectively into
HF^{2k}(H_{P^{-}})
(to kill classes), so

r^{2k}=|H_{2k-1}(B)|-K^{2k}\leq|HF^{2k}(H_{P^{-}})|-K^{2k}=|QH^{2k}(Y)|-\delta_{P^{-}}^{2k}-K^{2k}.

We now justify the third inequality line:
B
can kill at most
|H_{2k-1}(B)|
classes in
QH^{2k}(Y)
, and the actual number of killed classes is
|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\gamma}|=|\ker\psi_{0^{+},\gamma}|
by definition, which is at least
\Delta_{+}^{2k}
by
1.32
.
For the final inequality,
H_{2k-1}(B)
kills classes of
HF^{2k}(H_{P^{-}})
but must allow
|QH^{2k}(Y)|-|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{P^{-}}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)|
independent classes from
QH^{2k}(Y)
to survive, by definition, so:

r\leq|HF^{2k}(H_{P^{-}})|-(|QH^{2k}(Y)|-|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{P^{-}}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)|)=|\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{P^{-}}\cap QH^{2k}(Y)|-\delta_{P^{-}}^{2k}.

The statement about the direct limit follows from
6.13
:
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
, and
HF^{*}_{+}(H_{\gamma})
stabilises to
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
for
\gamma\gg k
because new generators appear in grading
\ll k
.
∎

Appendix A
Grading for Hamiltonian Floer theory

For gradings, we follow the conventions in
[
MR23
, App.C]
, and we refer the reader to
[
Sal97
,
Gut14
]
for a detailed discussion and references about the Robbin–Salamon index
[
RS95
]
.

Gradings of non-degenerate 1-periodic Hamiltonian orbits
144
144

144

I.e. 1-periodic orbits
x(t)
of
X_{H}
satisfying
ker((\phi_{1}^{H})_{*}-Id)_{x(0)}=0,
where
\phi_{t}^{H}
is the Hamiltonian flow of
H.

are defined using the Conley-Zehnder index. This is a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued index defined for certain non-degenerate paths of symplectic matrices. However, for Hamiltonians that have degenerate orbits (e.g. autonomous Hamiltonians), one uses the more general
Robbin–Salamon index

[
RS95
]
. The latter is a
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-valued index defined for
any
continuous path
[0,1]\rightarrow Sp(\mathbb{C}^{n},\Omega_{0})
of real symplectic matrices in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the standard real symplectic structure
\Omega_{0}
. We recall here the main properties that we will need. Denote by
\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{m}
the direct sum of two symplectic matrices
\psi_{1}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\psi_{2}:\mathbb{C}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m}.

Theorem A.1
.

The Robbin--Salamon index satisfies the following properties:
145
145

145

We follow
[
MR23
, App.C]
but abbreviated
\mathbb{W}(x)=W(2\pi x)
compared to the function
W
in that paper.

(1)

\mu_{RS}
is invariant under homotopies with fixed endpoints.

(2)

\mu_{RS}
is additive under concatenation of paths.

(3)

\mu_{RS}
is compatible with sums:
\mu_{RS}(\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2})=\mu_{RS}(\psi_{1})+\mu_{RS}(\psi_{2}).

(4)

Consider two continuous paths of symplectic matrices
\psi,\phi:[0,1]\rightarrow Sp(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\Omega_{0}).
Then

\mu_{RS}(\phi\psi\phi^{-1})=\mu_{RS}(\psi).

(5)

\mu_{RS}((e^{2\pi is})_{s\in[0,x]})=\mathbb{W}(x),
where

(93)

\mathbb{W}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Z},\quad\mathbb{W}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2\lfloor x\rfloor+1&\text{if}\ x\notin\mathbb{Z}\\
2x&\text{if}\ x\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{array}\right.

(6)

The Robbin–Salamon index of the symplectic shear

\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
b(t)&1\end{bmatrix}
is equal to
\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sign}(b(1))-\mathrm{sign}(b(0))).

We remark the following basic properties of the function
\mathbb{W}
:

(94)

\mathbb{W}(0)=0,\;\;\quad\mathbb{W}(-x)=-\mathbb{W}(x),\;\;\quad\mathbb{W}(x)\textrm{ is odd except at }\mathbb{Z},\;\;\quad 2x\geq\mathbb{W}(x)-1\geq 2x-2.

For any 1-orbit
x
of a Hamiltonian
H
in a symplectic manifold
M
of dimension
2n
, let
\phi_{t}
be the Hamiltonian flow and consider its linearisation

(\phi_{t})_{*}:T_{x(0)}M\rightarrow T_{x(t)}M
.
We pick a symplectic trivialisation
\Phi:x^{*}TM\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}\times S^{1},

of the tangent bundle above the orbit
x
to get a path of symplectic matrices
\psi(t)=\Phi_{t}\circ(\phi_{t})_{*}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{0}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Then define

(95)

RS(x,H):=\mu_{RS}(\psi).

This may depend on the choice of trivialisation
\Phi
. When
c_{1}(M)=0,
one can trivialise the canonical bundle
\Lambda^{n,0}(T^{*}M),
and then choose a trivialisation
\Phi
compatible with it. If in addition
H^{1}(M)=0,
then all trivialisations of
\Lambda^{n,0}(T^{*}M)
are equivalent, so the indices (
95
) are canonical
[
Sei08
, Sec.(3a)]
.

We define the
grading
of a 1-orbit
x
of a Hamiltonian
H:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(96)

|x|:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}M-RS(x,H).

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold with
c_{1}(TM,I)=0
for an an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
I
.
Then Floer cohomology
HF^{*}(H)
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, and canonically so if

H^{1}(M)=0
.

Appendix B
Cotangent bundles and negative vector bundles

B.1.
The moment map is a function of the radial coordinate for
T^{*}X

Recall that
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}
can be seen as the hyperkähler reduction of the flat space

M:=\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the action

G:=U(1)\curvearrowright M
given by

g\cdot(z,\xi)=(zg^{-1},g\xi).

The hyperkähler moment map
\mu=(\mu_{\mathbb{R}},\mu_{\mathbb{C}})
has real part

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}=\xi\xi^{*}-z^{*}z

and complex part

\mu_{\mathbb{C}}=\xi z
(viewing
\xi
as a row vector).
Taking
\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}>0
, the hyperkähler reduction gives

\mathfrak{M}:=\mu^{-1}(-\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Id},0)/G\cong T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}.

It admits an
S^{1}
-action induced by the action
t\cdot(z,\xi)=(z,t\xi)
on
M,

whose moment map is given by

H=\mathrm{tr}(\xi\xi^{*}).

There is a projection

\Psi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{n},\ [(z,\xi)]\mapsto z\xi,

which is an example of a Springer resolution.
This map, together with the
S^{1}
-invariant Kähler structure on
\mathfrak{M}
(induced from the Kähler structure on
M
)
makes
\mathfrak{M}
a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold globally defined over the convex base
B=\mathfrak{sl}_{n}.

The pull-back of the radial coordinate on
B
is
thus equal to

(97)

\Phi=\mathrm{tr}(z\xi(z\xi)^{*})=\mathrm{tr}(z\xi\xi^{*}z^{*})=\mathrm{tr}(z^{*}z\xi\xi^{*}).

Substituting the moment map equation

\mu_{\mathbb{R}}=\xi\xi^{*}-z^{*}z=-\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Id}

into (
97
)
we get

(98)

\displaystyle\Phi=\mathrm{tr}((\xi\xi^{*}+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Id})\xi\xi^{*})=\mathrm{tr}(\xi\xi^{*}\xi\xi^{*})+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{tr}(\xi\xi^{*})=\mathrm{tr}(\xi\xi^{*}\xi\xi^{*})+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}H.

Now, noticing that
A:=\xi\xi^{*}
is actually a
1\times 1
-matrix, the last term in
(
98
)
becomes

(99)

\Phi=\mathrm{tr}(A^{2})+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}H=\mathrm{tr}(A)^{2}+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}H=H^{2}+\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}H,

thus
146
146

146

Since
H\geq 0,
the other solution of the quadratic equation is invalid.

H=-\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}+\sqrt{\Phi+\frac{1}{4}\zeta_{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}

is indeed a function of
\Phi.

We remark that the same conclusion does not hold for cotangent bundles of other flag varieties of type A, with the symplectic structure from the analogous hyperkähler reduction.
147
147

147

Constructed e.g. in
[
Nak94
, Sec.7]
.

For example, to get the cotangent bundle of a Grassmannian,
T^{*}Gr(k,n),
one has to change
z
and
\xi
to
(n\times k)
and
(k\times n)
-matrices. Then
A=\xi\xi^{*}
is a
(k\times k)
-matrix, so the
\mathrm{tr}(A^{2})=\mathrm{tr}(A)^{2}
needed in (
99
) fails.

B.2.
T^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
and negative vector bundles

A vector bundle
E
over a compact manifold
X
is
negative
in the weak sense of
[
Rit14
, Lem.70]
if
one can build a (real) symplectic form on
E
of type

\omega=\pi^{*}\omega_{X}+\Omega,

determined by a choice of Hermitian connection on
E
, where
\Omega|_{\textrm{fibre}}=\textrm{(area form)}/\pi
in a unitary frame, and
\Omega
on horizontal vectors is determined by the curvature form of
E\to X
.
148
148

148

Moreover
\Omega(TX,\cdot)=0
, and
\Omega(v,h)=0
if
v
is vertical and
h
is horizontal.

As explained in the proof of
[
Rit14
, Lem.70]
, it suffices
149
149

149

Key observation:
\omega:=\pi^{*}\omega_{X}+\Omega
on the total space
\mathrm{Tot}(E\to X)
is symplectic because of the positivity of the form

\tfrac{1}{2\pi i}w^{\dagger}\mathcal{F}^{E}_{(\pi_{*}h,I\pi_{*}h)}w=\tfrac{r^{2}}{2\pi i}\mathcal{F}_{(h,Ih)}^{L}>0,

for
w\in E\setminus\{0\}
, for horizontal
h\neq 0\in T_{w}E
(thus a horizontal vector of the projection
{\mathbb{P}}(E)\to X
), where
r=|w|
is the radial coordinate for the fibres of
E
. Remark: the conventions
[
Rit14
, Sec.11.2]
are that the “radial coordinate” for
L
viewed as a convex symplectic manifold is
R^{L}=(1+r^{2})/2
(whose moment map generates the flow
e^{\pi it}
rather than
e^{2\pi it}
), so the Hamiltonians used for Floer theory on
E
are functions
c(2R^{L})
that become linear in
R^{L}
at infinity.

that the natural complex line bundle
L:=L(E)\to\mathbb{P}(E)
over the complex projectivisation of
E
is a negative line bundle.
150
150

150

A complex line bundle
L\to B
over a symplectic manifold is negative in the sense of
[
Rit14
, Sec.7]
if
c_{1}(L)
is a negative multiple of
[\omega_{B}]
. This is equivalent to the existence of a Hermitian metric and a compatible Hermitian connection such that the curvature induces a form

-\tfrac{i}{2\pi}\mathcal{F}^{L}
(which represents
-c_{1}(L)
) which
is positive on complex lines for any choice of
\omega_{B}
-compatible almost complex structure. In the context of holomorphic line bundles, negativity implies that a choice of Hermitian metric yields a Chern connection whose curvature determines a Kähler form on
B
via
-\tfrac{i}{2\pi}\mathcal{F}^{L}
.

If
E
is Griffiths weak-negative
[
Gri65
]
then this condition on
L
holds
[
Gri65
, Prop.9.1]
. Example:
E=T^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is Griffiths weak-negative for the Fubini–Study metric
[
Gri65
, Sec.10.(iii)]
, so it is negative in the above sense.

Negative vector bundles arise for example after twisting
E\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\otimes N}
any complex vector bundle
E
by a large enough power of a negative line bundle
\mathcal{L}

[
Rit14
, Cor.9]
(see also

[
Gri65
, Prop.4.3]
).
A class of examples of negative holomorphic vector bundles are duals of ample vector bundles
[
Gri65
, Prop.7.3]
, e.g. the conormal
151
151

151

the dual of the normal bundle, so sections are cotangent vectors of the ambient that vanish on
TV
.
bundle to any projective algebraic variety
V\subset\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{n}

[
Gri65
, Corollary in Sec.10]
. The fact that
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n}
is weak-negative is deduced from the ampleness of

B.3.
The filtration for negative vector bundles

We now prove
1.39
.
By
[
Rit14
, Thm.72]
,
Q_{\varphi}=c:=\pi^{*}c_{\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{C}}E}(E)
(with a possible rescaling by an invertible element of the form
1+T^{>0}\in\mathbb{K}
in the Calabi–Yau setting). Indeed, this holds more generally if
E
satisfies weak+ monotonicity.
The
S^{1}
-periods are integers, so by the stability property in (
15
), the filtration can only jump when full-rotations occur, as explained in
Section

1.10
.
Recall
E_{0}
from
Footnote

4
is the generalised
0
-eigenspace of quantum product by
c
, so
E_{0}=\ker c^{m}
if
x^{m}
is the highest factor of
x
in the minimal polynomial of quantum product by
c
on
QH^{*}(Y)
.
A basic observation from linear algebra is that the kernels of powers of an endomorphism on a finite dimensional vector space will strictly increase until they stabilise. Thus
\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
strictly increases in size precisely at
\lambda=1,2,\ldots,m
. In the Calabi-Yau setting,
c
is nilpotent (e.g. because
0=SH^{*}(Y)=QH^{*}(Y)_{c}
), so
\mathscr{F}_{m}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)
.

Consider
\ker c^{N}\subset H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
, and the notation from
Section

1.7
.
Let
q=n_{0}+T^{>0}\textrm{-terms}\in QH^{*}(Y)
, so
n_{0}=\mathrm{ini}(q)\in H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
. Then
c\star q=0
implies the lowest order
T
-term vanishes:
0=\mathrm{ini}(c)\cup\mathrm{ini}(q)=c\cup n_{0}.

Similarly,
c^{\star N}\star q=0\Rightarrow c^{N}\cup n_{0}=0
, so
\mathrm{ini}(\ker c^{\star N})\subset\ker c^{N}
, so
\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B},N}^{\varphi}\subset\ker c^{N}.

Now assume
\mathrm{Im}(c\star)\subset\mathrm{Im}(c\,\cup)
. Given
n_{0}\in\ker c
, we find
n_{1}T^{a_{1}}
, with
a_{1}>0
, such that
c\star n_{0}-c\,\cup n_{0}=c\,\cup n_{1}T^{a_{1}}.
Thus
c\star(n_{0}+T^{a_{1}}n_{1})=T^{>a_{1}}
-terms. By induction, one similarly builds
q=n_{0}+T^{a_{1}}n_{1}+T^{a_{2}}n_{2}+\cdots
, for strictly increasing
a_{j}
, so that
c\star q=0
and
\mathrm{ini}(q)=n_{0}
.
So
\mathrm{ini}(\ker(c\star))=\ker(c\,\cup)
.
The same holds for
c^{\star N}
and
c^{N}
, for
1\leq N\leq m
, using
\mathrm{Im}(c^{\star j}\star)\subset\mathrm{Im}(c^{j}\,\cup)
.
